\subsection{Generalized \texorpdfstring{$\boldsymbol{6j}$}{6j} symbols \& tetrahedral eigenvalues}
\label{sec:sixj_tet}

The purpose of this section is to introduce a representation theoretic object, namely the generalized Wigner $6j$ symbol, and then show how its asymptotic properties encode whether a given sextuple $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ is tetrahedral in a manner which analogous to how the Littlewood-Richardson coefficient encodes whether a given triple $(\ea,\eb,\ec)$ is trigonal.
For the case where $n=2$, this correspondence is well-known \cite{roberts1999classical,ponzano1968semiclassical,backens2010sixjsymbols}, while for the case where $n \geq 3$, this correspondence was already established indirectly by \citeauthor{christandl2018recoupling} \cite{christandl2018recoupling}.
Here we establish this correspondence directly.

\begin{defn}
    \label{defn:tet_gamma}
    Given positive integers $a,b$, and $d$, let $c=a+b,f=b+d$, and $e=a+b+d$.
    Let $(\ya, \yb, \yc, \yd, \ye, \yf)$ be a sextuple of Young diagrams with sizes:
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:six_yd_sizes}
        \ya \vdash a, \qquad
        \yb \vdash b, \qquad
        \yc \vdash c, \qquad
        \yd \vdash d, \qquad
        \ye \vdash e, \qquad
        \yf \vdash f.
    \end{equation}
    Define corresponding projection operators acting on $(\mathbb C^{n})^{\ot a+b+d}$ by
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:tet_extended_projs}
        \begin{alignedat}{3}
            &\Pi_{\ya} \coloneqq P_{\ya} \ot \id^{\ot b} \ot \id^{\ot d}, \qquad
            &&\Pi_{\yb} \coloneqq \id^{\ot a} \ot P_{\yb} \ot \id^{\ot d}, \qquad
            &&\Pi_{\yc} \coloneqq P_{\yc} \ot \id^{\ot d}, \qquad\\
             &\Pi_{\yd} \coloneqq \id^{\ot a} \ot \id^{\ot b} \ot P_{\yd}, \qquad
            &&\Pi_{\ye} \coloneqq P_{\ye}, \qquad
            &&\Pi_{\yf} \coloneqq \id^{\ot a} \ot P_{\yf}.
        \end{alignedat}
    \end{equation}
    Given $n\times n$ positive semidefinite matrices $A,B,D$ with $C=A+B$, $F=B+D$, and $E=A+B+D$, let
    \begin{equation}
        \TetGamma
        = \Tr[(\nrm A^{\ot a} \ot \nrm B^{\ot b} \ot \nrm D^{\ot d}) \Pi_{\ya}\Pi_{\yb}\Pi_{\yc}\Pi_{\yd}\Pi_{\ye}\Pi_{\yf}].
    \end{equation}
\end{defn}

\begin{rem}
    In the analysis that follows, it will be convenient to avoid certain edges cases pertaining to the candidate sextuple of positive semidefinite eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$.
    Specifically, let the extremal traces be denoted by
    \begin{equation}
        \tau_{\min} \coloneqq \min\{ \abs \ea, \abs \eb, \abs \ec, \abs \ed, \abs \ee, \abs \ef\},
        \qquad
        \tau_{\max} \coloneqq \max\{ \abs \ea, \abs \eb, \abs \ec, \abs \ed, \abs \ee, \abs \ef\},
    \end{equation}
    and the extremal ranks be denoted by
    \begin{equation}
        r_{\min} \coloneqq \min\{ r(\ea), r(\eb), r(\ec), r(\ed), r(\ee), r(\ef) \},
        \qquad
        r_{\max} \coloneqq \max\{ r(\ea), r(\eb), r(\ec), r(\ed), r(\ee), r(\ef) \}.
    \end{equation}
    In order to avoid the edge case where one of the eigenvalues $\ex \in \{\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef\}$ is totally zero, we sometimes demand that the minimum trace is strictly positive or equivalently that the minimum rank is at least one:
    \begin{equation}
        \tau_{\min} > 0, \qquad r_{\min} \geq 1.
    \end{equation}
    %In the context of the tetrahedral Horn problem for positive semidefinite eigenvalues, one necessarily has $\tau_{\min} = \min(\abs \ea, \abs \eb, \abs \ed)$.
\end{rem}

Another technical difficulty which complicates subsequent analysis is that the set of projection operators $\{\Pi_{\ya}, \Pi_{\yb}, \Pi_{\yc}, \Pi_{\yd}, \Pi_{\ye}, \Pi_{\yf}\}$ defined by \cref{eq:tet_extended_projs} is \textit{almost} pairwise mutually commuting.
\begin{lem}
    \label{lem:tet_com_rels}
    Every pair of projection operators in $\{\Pi_{\ya}, \Pi_{\yb}, \Pi_{\yc}, \Pi_{\yd}, \Pi_{\ye}, \Pi_{\yf}\}$ is commuting with only possible exception being $\Pi_{\yc}$ and $\Pi_{\yf}$.
    Therefore, the operators $\Pi^{\yc}_{\ya\yb\yd\ye}$ and $\Pi^{\yf}_{\ya\yb\yd\ye}$ defined by
    \begin{align}
        \label{eq:commuting_extended_projs}
        \begin{split}
            \Pi^{\yc}_{\ya\yb\yd\ye} = \Pi_{\yc}\Pi_{\ya}\Pi_{\yb}\Pi_{\yd}\Pi_{\ye}, \quad \text{and} \quad
            \Pi^{\yf}_{\ya\yb\yd\ye} = \Pi_{\yf}\Pi_{\ya}\Pi_{\yb}\Pi_{\yd}\Pi_{\ye},
        \end{split}
    \end{align}
    are projection operators.
\end{lem}
\begin{proof}
    That all projection operators except the pair $\{\Pi_{\yc}, \Pi_{\yf}\}$ commute follows from elementary applications of Schur's lemma.
    The obstruction for the pair $\{\Pi_{\yc}, \Pi_{\yf}\}$ is simply that $\Pi_{\yc}$ is not necessarily $\SU(n)$-equivariant with respect to the diagonal action on $\mathbb C^{\ot b+d}$ and likewise $\Pi_{\yf}$ is not necessarily $\SU(n)$-equivariant with respect to the diagonal action on $\mathbb C^{\ot a+b}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{defn}[Generalized 6j operators]
    \label{defn:sixj}
    Let $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ be a sextuple of Young diagrams with sizes $(a,b,c,d,e,f)$ given by \cref{eq:six_yd_sizes}.
    Then we define the \defnsty{$\U(n)$-$6j$ operator} to be the operator\footnotemark{}
    \begin{equation}
        \abcdefsymbol
        :
        C^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb} \ot C^{\ye}_{\yd,\yc} \to C^{\ye}_{\ya, \yf} \ot C^{\yf}_{\yb,\yd},
    \end{equation}
    defined by
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:sixj}
        \abcdefsymbol
        \coloneqq
        \frac{1}{\dim(V_{\ye})}
        \Tr_{V_{\ye}}[
        Y_{\ya,\yf}^{\ye\dagger}
        (\id_{V_{\ya}}\ot Y_{\yb,\yd}^{\yf\dagger})
        (Y_{\ya,\yb}^{\yc}\ot\id_{V_{\yd}})
        Y_{\yc,\yd}^{\ye}
        ],
    \end{equation}
    and depicted diagrammatically by
    \begin{equation}
        \tikzfig{tikz/final/sixj_defn}.
    \end{equation}
    \footnotetext{It is important to note that there does not seem to exist a universal convention concerning how $6j$ symbols are normalized; \citeauthor{coquereaux2023integer} discusses is problem in \cite{coquereaux2023integer}.}.
\end{defn}

The following proposition is the analogue of \cref{prop:HS_integral_uni}.
\begin{prop}
    \label{prop:HS_integral_tet}
    Let $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ be Young diagrams with sizes given by \cref{eq:six_yd_sizes}.
    Then
    \begin{align}
        \label{eq:tet_mean_value}
        \begin{split}
            \mathbb E_{A,B,D}\TetGamma
            &\coloneqq
            \iiint \diff \mu(A)\diff \mu(B)\diff \mu(D)\TetGamma\\
            &= h^n_\ya h^n_\yb h^n_\yd \frac{\dim V_\ye^n}{\dim(V_{\ya}^n \ot V_{\yb}^n \ot V_{\yd}^n)}
            \norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{2}^2.
        \end{split}
    \end{align}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    To prove \cref{eq:tet_mean_value}, we use \cref{prop:HS_measure} and \cref{lem:tet_com_rels} to see that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:tet_mean_step_1}
        \mathbb E_{A,B,D}\TetGamma = q_{\ya}q_{\yb}q_{\yd} \Tr(\Pi_{\ya}\Pi_{\yb}\Pi_{\yc}\Pi_{\yd}\Pi_{\ye}\Pi_{\yf}).
    \end{equation}
    We claim that the product $\Pi_{\ya}\Pi_{\yb}\Pi_{\yc}\Pi_{\yd}\Pi_{\ye}\Pi_{\yf}$ is related to the generalized $6j$ operator $\abcdefsymbol$ in that there exists appropriately-typed isometries $\iota_{I}$ and $\iota_{II}$ such that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:ud_sixj_prod_proj_iso}
        \Pi_{\ya}\Pi_{\yb}\Pi_{\yc}\Pi_{\yd}\Pi_{\ye}\Pi_{\yf}
        =
        \iota_{II} \left(\id_{V_{\ee}^{n}} \ot \id_{W_{\ea}} \ot \id_{W_{\eb}} \ot \id_{W_{\ed}} \ot \abcdefsymbol \right) \iota_{I}^{\dagger}
    \end{equation}
    The form of these isometries can be deduced by applying \cref{eq:lr_triple_prod} four times in the following diagrammatic calculation:
    \begin{equation}
        \scalebox{0.9}{\tikzfig{tikz/final/sixj_sextuple_prod}}.
    \end{equation}
    From \cref{eq:ud_sixj_prod_proj_iso} we conclude
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:ud_sixj_prod_proj_hs_norm}
        \Tr(\Pi_{\ya}\Pi_{\yb}\Pi_{\yc}\Pi_{\yd}\Pi_{\ye}\Pi_{\yf}) = \dim(V_{\ee}^{n})\dim(W_{\ea})\dim(W_{\eb})\dim(W_{\ed})\norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{2}^2,
    \end{equation}
    Combining \cref{eq:ud_sixj_prod_proj_hs_norm} with \cref{eq:tet_mean_step_1} and \cref{eq:HS_mean_coeff} then yields \cref{eq:tet_mean_value}.
\end{proof}
The following proposition is the analogue of \cref{prop:completeness_rel_uni}.
\begin{prop}
    \label{prop:completeness_rel_tet}
    Let $(A,B,C,D,E,F)$ be tetrahedral positive semidefinite matrices with eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$.
    Then
    \begin{align}
        \label{eq:tet_completeness}
        \sum_{\substack{\ya \yb \yc \\ \yd \ye \yf}}
        \TetGamma = 1.
    \end{align}
    where the sum is taken over Young diagrams with the following sizes:
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:six_yd_sizes_rank}
        \ya \vdash_{r(\ea)} a, \qquad
        \yb \vdash_{r(\eb)} b, \qquad
        \yc \vdash_{r(\ec)} c, \qquad
        \yd \vdash_{r(\ed)} d, \qquad
        \ye \vdash_{r(\ee)} e, \qquad
        \yf \vdash_{r(\ef)} f.
    \end{equation}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    To begin, note that \cref{eq:tet_completeness} holds when the sum is taken over Young diagrams with unrestricted rank according to \cref{eq:six_yd_sizes} because $\sum_{\yx \vdash_n \ell} P_{\yx} = \id_{n}^{\otimes \ell}$ for all positive integers $\ell$ and $\yx \in \{\ya, \yb, \yc, \yd, \ye, \yf\}$.
    That one can restrict to Young diagrams with depths bounded according to \cref{eq:six_yd_sizes_rank} follows
    because $\TetGamma = 0$ whenever the depth of \textit{any} of $(\ya, \yb, \yc, \yd, \ye, \yf)$ exceeds the corresponding ranks $(r(\ea), r(\eb), r(\ec), r(\ed), r(\ee), r(\ef))$.\footnote{This claim actually follows from \cref{thm:abd_dep_upper_bound} and will be proven shortly. Importantly, \cref{thm:abd_dep_upper_bound} does not rely on \cref{prop:completeness_rel_tet}.}
\end{proof}

Our next goal will be to derive two upper bounds on the absolute value of $\TetGamma$.
Our first upper bound is \textit{independent} of the given matrices and follows as a corollary of \cref{lem:tet_com_rels}.
\begin{cor}
    \label{cor:abd_indep_upper_bound}
    Let $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ be Young diagrams of sizes given by \cref{eq:six_yd_sizes}.
    Then for all tetrahedral, non-zero positive semidefinite $(A,B,C,D,E,F)$, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:tet_gamma_sixj_upper_bound}
        \abs{\TetGamma}
        \leq \norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{\infty}.
    \end{equation}
\end{cor}
\begin{proof}
    From \cref{eq:commuting_extended_projs} we have
    \begin{equation}
        \TetGamma
        =
        \Tr(\Pi^{\yf}_{\ya\yb\yd\ye}(\nrm A^{\ot a} \ot \nrm B^{\ot b} \ot \nrm D^{\ot d}) \Pi^{\yc}_{\ya\yb\yd\ye}).
    \end{equation}
    Moreover, as $\Omega \coloneqq \nrm A^{\ot a} \ot \nrm B^{\ot b} \ot \nrm D^{\ot d}$ is positive semidefinite trace one,
    and $\Pi_1 \coloneqq \Pi^{\yf}_{\ya\yb\yd\ye}$ and $\Pi_2 \coloneqq \Pi^{\yc}_{\ya\yb\yd\ye}$ are projection operators by \cref{lem:tet_com_rels}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \abs{\TetGamma} = \abs{\Tr(\Pi_1 \Omega \Pi_2)} \leq \abs{\braket{v, \Pi_1 \Pi_2 v}} \leq \norm{\Pi_1\Pi_2}_{\infty} = \norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{\infty}.
    \end{equation}
    where the last equality follows from the isometry decomposition provided by \cref{eq:ud_sixj_prod_proj_iso}.
\end{proof}

Our second upper bound on the magnitude of $\TetGamma$ depends on the \textit{eigenvalues} $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ of the given tetrahedral matrices $(A, B, C, D, E, F)$ where $C=A+B$, $F=B+D$ and $E=A+B+D$.
This result proves that in the limit of large Young diagrams, the magnitude of $\TetGamma$ is sharply concentrated around those Young diagrams $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ whose shapes resemble the sextuple of eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ of $(A,B,C,D,E,F)$.
\begin{thm}
    \label{thm:abd_dep_upper_bound}
    Let everything be as in \cref{defn:tet_gamma} and let the Young diagrams $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ have sizes given by
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:ceil_yd_sizes}
        \begin{alignedat}{3}
            &\ya \vdash a = \ceil{k\abs{\ea}}, \qquad
            &&\yb \vdash b = \ceil{k\abs{\eb}}, \qquad
            &&\yc \vdash c = \ceil{k\abs{\ea}}+\ceil{k\abs{\eb}}, \qquad\\
            &\yd \vdash d = \ceil{k\abs{\ed}}, \qquad
            &&\ye \vdash e = \ceil{k\abs{\ea}}+\ceil{k\abs{\eb}}+\ceil{k\abs{\ed}}, \qquad
            &&\yf \vdash f = \ceil{k\abs{\eb}}+\ceil{k\abs{\ed}},
        \end{alignedat}
    \end{equation}
    for some positive integer $k > \tau_{\min}^{-1}$ where $\tau_{\min}$ is the minimum trace.
    Then
    \begin{equation}
        \abs{\TetGamma}
        \leq p(k) \exp(-k I^{\ya\yb\yc\yd\ye\yf}_{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}/6).
        \label{eq:tet_gamma_upper_bound}
    \end{equation}
    where $p(k)$ grows polynomially in $k$ and where $I^{\ya\yb\yc\yd\ye\yf}_{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}$ is a quantity which measures the deviation of the primed quantities from the unprimed quantities:
    \begin{equation}
        I^{\ya\yb\yc\yd\ye\yf}_{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}
        =\abs{\ea} \kl{\nya}{\nea}
        +\abs{\eb} \kl{\nyb}{\neb}
        +\abs{\ec} \kl{\nyc}{\nec}
        +\abs{\ed} \kl{\nyd}{\ned}
        +\abs{\ee} \kl{\nye}{\nee}
        +\abs{\ef} \kl{\nyf}{\nef}.
    \end{equation}
    %\textcolor{red}{TODO: Is this relative entropy deviation measure conceptually useful or is it best to relax the deviation measure to its geometric analogue using Pinsker's inequality?}
\end{thm}
\begin{proof}
    The first portion of the proof does not depend on the value of $k$.
    For now, we suppose the Young diagrams $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ have sizes determined by \cref{eq:six_yd_sizes}.
    As in the proof of \cref{cor:abd_indep_upper_bound}, we let $\Omega \coloneqq \nrm A^{\ot a} \ot \nrm B^{\ot b} \ot \nrm D^{\ot d}$.
    Using the commutation relations provided by \cref{lem:tet_com_rels} we apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to $\braket{X,Y} = \Tr(X^{*} Y) \leq \Tr(X^{*}X)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Tr(Y^{*}Y)^{\frac{1}{2}}$ for $X = \Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{\yf}\Pi_{\ya}\Pi_{\yb}$ and $Y = \Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{\yc} \Pi_{\yd} \Pi_{\ye}$ to obtain
    \begin{align}
        \begin{split}
            \abs{\TetGamma}^{6}
            &=\abs{\Tr[(\Pi_{\yf}\Pi_{\ya}\Pi_{\yb}) \Omega (\Pi_{\yc} \Pi_{\yd} \Pi_{\ye})]}^6\\
            &\leq
            \Tr(\Pi_{\yf}\Pi_{\ya}\Pi_{\yb} \Omega)^3
            \Tr(\Pi_{\yc}\Pi_{\yd}\Pi_{\ye} \Omega)^3\\
            &\leq
            \Tr(\Pi_{\yf}\Omega)\Tr(\Pi_{\ya}\Omega)\Tr(\Pi_{\yb} \Omega)
            \Tr(\Pi_{\yc}\Omega)\Tr(\Pi_{\yd}\Omega)\Tr(\Pi_{\ye} \Omega),
        \end{split}
    \end{align}
    where the last inequality follows because $\Pi_{\yx} \leq \id_n^{\ot \abs\yx}$ for each $\yx$ in $\{\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf\}$.

    Now, for each $\yx \in \{\ya, \yb, \yc, \yd, \ye, \yf\}$, it is possible to upper-bound $\Tr(\Pi_{\yx} \Omega)$ using the eigenvalue estimation technique (\cref{prop:eig_est}).
    For a detailed example, we will consider value of $\Tr(\Pi_{\yc} \Omega)$:
    \begin{align}
        \Tr(\Pi_{\yc} \Omega)
        = \frac{\Tr[(P_{\yc} \ot \id^{\ot d})(A^{\ot a}\ot B^{\ot b} \ot D^{\ot d})]}{\abs{\ea}^{a}\abs{\eb}^{b}\abs{\ed}^{d}}
        = \frac{\Tr[P_{\yc}(A^{\ot a}\ot B^{\ot b})]}{\abs{\ea}^{a}\abs{\eb}^{b}}.
    \end{align}
    Under the assumption that $C = A+B$, we have $C^{\ot c} = (A+B)^{\ot a+b}$ which implies
    \begin{equation}
        \Tr(P_{\yc} C^{\ot c}) = \sum_{k=0}^{c} \binom{c}{k} \Tr(P_{\yc} (A^{\ot k} \ot B^{\ot c-k}).
    \end{equation}
    Here we have used the $S_{c}$-equivariance of $P_{\yc}$.
    Since each of the summands above are non-negative, we conclude for all $0 \leq j \leq c$ that
    \begin{equation}
        \Tr(P_{\yc} C^{\ot c}) \geq \binom{c}{j} \Tr(P_{\yc} (A^{\ot j} \ot B^{\ot c-j}).
    \end{equation}
    Letting $j = a$ and rearranging yields
    \begin{equation}
        \Tr(P_{\yc} (A^{\ot a} \ot B^{\ot b})) \leq \frac{a!b!}{c!}\Tr(P_{\yc} C^{\ot c}).
    \end{equation}
    Combining this result with the spectrum estimation bound (\cref{prop:eig_est}) yields
    \begin{align}
        \Tr(\Pi_{\yc} \Omega)
        %&\leq \frac{1}{\abs{\ea}^{a}\abs{\eb}^{b}}\frac{x!y!}{(x+y)!}\Tr[P_{\yc}C^{\ot a+y}],
        \leq \frac{\abs{\ec}^{c}}{\abs{\ea}^{a}\abs{\eb}^{b}}\frac{a!b!}{c!}\dim(V_{\yc}^{n})\exp(-c\kl{\nyc}{\nec}).
    \end{align}
    Analogous calculations for $\yx \in \{\ya, \yb, \yc, \yd, \ye, \yf\}$ produce the following set of upper bounds:
    \begin{align}
        \begin{split}
            \Tr(\Pi_{\ya} \Omega)
            &\leq \dim(V_{\ya}^{n}) \exp(- a \kl{\nya}{\nea}), \\
            \Tr(\Pi_{\yb} \Omega)
            &\leq \dim(V_{\yb}^{n}) \exp(- b \kl{\nyb}{\neb}), \\
            \Tr(\Pi_{\yc} \Omega)
            &\leq \dim(V_{\yc}^{n})\frac{\abs{\ec}^{c}}{\abs{\ea}^{a}\abs{\eb}^{b}}\frac{a!b!}{c!} \exp(- c \kl{\nyc}{\nec}), \\
            \Tr(\Pi_{\yd} \Omega)
            &\leq \dim(V_{\yd}^{n}) \exp(- d \kl{\nyd}{\ned}), \\
            \Tr(\Pi_{\ye} \Omega)
            &\leq \dim(V_{\ye}^{n})\frac{\abs{\ee}^{e}}{\abs{\ea}^{a}\abs{\eb}^{b}\abs{\ed}^{d}}\frac{a!b!d!}{e!} \exp(- e \kl{\nye}{\nee}), \\
            \Tr(\Pi_{\yf} \Omega)
            &\leq \dim(V_{\yf}^{n}) \frac{\abs{\ef}^{f}}{\abs{\eb}^{b}\abs{\ed}^{d}}\frac{b!d!}{f!} \exp(- f \kl{\nyf}{\nef}).
        \end{split}
    \end{align}
    Our objective now will be to use the $k$-dependent values for $(a,b,c,d,e,f)$ to upper bound the multinomial prefactors appearing in the bounds for $\yc$, $\ye$, and $\yf$ above in terms of $k$.
    From \cref{eq:ceil_yd_sizes}, we obtain the following estimates:
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:size_estimates}
        \begin{alignedat}{3}
            &k \abs{\ea} \leq a < k \abs{\ea} + 1, \qquad
            &&k \abs{\eb} \leq b < k \abs{\eb} + 1, \qquad
            &&k \abs{\ec} \leq c < k \abs{\ec} + 2, \\
            &k \abs{\ed} \leq d < k \abs{\ed} + 1, \qquad
            &&k \abs{\ee} \leq e < k \abs{\ee} + 3, \qquad
            &&k \abs{\ef} \leq f < k \abs{\ef} + 2.
        \end{alignedat}
    \end{equation}
    An application of \cref{lem:multinomial_dist_lower_bound} for binomial distributions ($m=2$) implies the following bound
    \begin{equation}
        \frac{\abs{\ec}^{c}}{\abs{\ea}^{a}\abs{\eb}^{b}}\frac{a!b!}{c!}
        \leq 12^{2}\left(k \abs{\ec}+2\right)^\frac{1}{2}.
    \end{equation}
    Moreover, from Weyl's dimension formula, \cref{eq:weyl_dim}, we obtain \cite[Equation 7]{christandl2006spectra}:
    \begin{equation}
        \dim(V_{\yc}^{n}) \leq (\abs{\yc}+1)^{n(n-1)/2} \leq (k\abs{\ec}+3)^{n(n-1)/2},
    \end{equation}
    because $(\yc_i - i)-(\yc_j - j) \leq \abs{\yc} + 1$ as well as $j-i\geq 1$.
    Altogether, we can relax the bound on $\Tr(\Pi_{\yc} \Omega)$ to obtain
    \begin{equation}
        \Tr(\Pi_{\yc} \Omega) \leq
        12^{2}(k\abs{\ec}+3)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}+\frac{1}{2}}\exp\left(-k\abs{\ec}\kl{\nyc}{\nec}\right).
    \end{equation}
    A similar calculations for $\Tr(\Pi_{\ye} \Omega)$ yields (noting to apply \cref{lem:multinomial_dist_lower_bound} for trinomial distributions when $m=3$) yields:
    \begin{equation}
        \Tr(\Pi_{\ye} \Omega) \leq 12^{3}(k\abs{\ee}+4)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}+1}\exp\left(-k\abs{\ee}\kl{\nye}{\nee}\right).
    \end{equation}
    It is also not too difficult to see that the polynomial in $k$ prefactor in the upper bound on $\Tr(\Pi_{\ye} \Omega)$ above is the largest such prefactor and thus \cref{eq:tet_gamma_upper_bound} indeed holds if we take $p(k)$ to be
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:p_of_k}
        p(k) = 12^{3}(k\tau_{\max}+4)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}+1}.
    \end{equation}
\end{proof}

While \cref{thm:abd_dep_upper_bound} states that $\TetGamma$ concentrates in magnitude around particular Young diagrams, it is not directly applicable to the tetrahedral Horn problem simply because its value depends on more than just the eigenvalues of the matrices $(A,B,C,D,E,F)$.
Nevertheless, combining \cref{thm:abd_dep_upper_bound} with the completeness relation (\cref{eq:tet_completeness}) yields a necessary condition for $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ to be tetrahedral.
\begin{cor}
    \label{cor:tet_sixj_lower_bound}
    Let $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ be the eigenvalues of non-zero positive semidefinite tetrahedral Hermitian matrices $(A,B,C,D,E,F)$ with minimum trace $\tau_{\min}$.
    Then there exists a sextuple of sequences of Young diagrams $(\ya_k, \yb_k, \yc_k, \yd_k, \ye_k, \yf_k)$ indexed by positive integers $k > \tau_{\min}^{-1}$ with sizes given by \cref{eq:ceil_yd_sizes}
    satisfying both (i) the convergence condition
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:sextuple_convergence_rate}
        I^{\ya_k\yb_k\yc_k\yd_k\ye_k\yf_k}_{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}
        \leq \frac{6}{k} \log p(k) q(k)
        \stackrel{k\to\infty}{\longrightarrow} 0,
    \end{equation}
    and (ii) the lower bound
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:ud_sixj_lower_bound}
        \norm{\tightcurlysixj{ \ya_k & \yb_k & \yc_k \\ \yd_k &  \ye_k &  \yf_k}}_{\infty}
        \geq \frac{1}{q(k)},
    \end{equation}
    where $p(k)$ is given by \cref{eq:p_of_k} whereas $q(k)$ is given by
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:q_of_k}
        q(k) = (k \tau_{\max} +4)^{6(r_{\max}-1)}.
    \end{equation}
\end{cor}
\begin{proof}
    To proof the claim, we construct the sequence implicitly.
    For each $k$, it suffices to take $(\ya_k, \yb_k, \yc_k, \yd_k, \ye_k, \yf_k)$ to be any sextuple of Young diagrams which maximizes the value of $\abs{\TetGamma}$ with sizes determined by \cref{eq:ceil_yd_sizes}:
    \begin{equation}
        (\ya_k, \yb_k, \yc_k, \yd_k, \ye_k, \yf_k) \coloneqq \underset{\ya\yb\yc\yd\ye\yf}{\argmax} \abs{\TetGamma}.
    \end{equation}
    While this maximal choice may not be unique, it will necessarily satisfy a lower bound due to the completeness relation satisfied by $\TetGamma$ \cref{eq:tet_completeness}.
    Specifically, this maximal choice must satisfy
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:tet_gamma_lower_bound}
        \abs{\TetGammak} \geq \frac{1}{q(k)}
    \end{equation}
    where $q(k)$ is any upper bound on the number of possible summands in the completeness relation \cref{eq:tet_completeness} viewed as a function of $k$.
    As there less than or equal to $(s+1)^{r-1}$ Young diagrams with $r$ rows with size at most $s$, letting $q(k)$ be given by \cref{eq:q_of_k} ensures \cref{eq:tet_gamma_lower_bound} holds because the size (resp. rank) of any of $(\ya_k, \yb_k, \yc_k, \yd_k, \ye_k, \yf_k)$ is at most $k \tau_{\max} + 3$ (resp. $r_{\max}$).
    Moreover, applying \cref{eq:tet_gamma_upper_bound} yields
    \begin{equation}
        I^{\ya_k\yb_k\yc_k\yd_k\ye_k\yf_k}_{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}
        \leq \frac{6}{k}\log (q(k)p(k))
    \end{equation}
    and thus \cref{eq:sextuple_convergence_rate} holds because $p(k)$ and $q(k)$ are polynomials in $k$ with coefficients depending on the maximum trace $\tau_{\max}$ and maximum rank $r_{\max}$.
    Also \cref{eq:ud_sixj_lower_bound} follows from \cref{eq:tet_gamma_lower_bound} combined with the upper bound provided by \cref{eq:tet_gamma_sixj_upper_bound}.
\end{proof}

\begin{rem}
    The convergence condition above (\cref{eq:sextuple_convergence_rate}) in conjunction with the size conditions given by \cref{eq:ceil_yd_sizes} together ensure that the Young diagrams $(\ya_k, \yb_k, \yc_k, \yd_k, \ye_k, \yf_k)$ converge to the eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ in the following sense that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:sextuple_convergence}
        \begin{alignedat}{3}
            & \frac{\ya_k}{k} \rightarrow \ea, \qquad
            && \frac{\yb_k}{k} \rightarrow \eb, \qquad
            && \frac{\yc_k}{k} \rightarrow \ec, \\
            & \frac{\yd_k}{k} \rightarrow \ed, \qquad
            && \frac{\ye_k}{k} \rightarrow \ee, \qquad
            && \frac{\yf_k}{k} \rightarrow \ef,
        \end{alignedat}
    \end{equation}
    where $\frac{\yx_k}{k} \rightarrow \ex$ is shorthand for $\lim_{k\to \infty} (\yx_k)_i/k = \ex_i$ for all $i \in \{1,\ldots, n\}$.
\end{rem}

While the existence of a convergent sequence $(\ya_k, \yb_k, \yc_k, \yd_k, \ye_k, \yf_k)$ of primed Young diagrams is necessary for $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ to be tetrahedral by \cref{cor:tet_sixj_lower_bound}, our next result proves it is also sufficient.
In other words, if $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ are not tetrahedral, then no such sequence can exist.

\begin{cor}
    Suppose $(\ea, \eb, \ec, \ed, \ee, \ef)$ is a non-tetrahedral sextuple of positive semidefinite and non-zero eigenvalues with minimum trace $\tau_{\min}$.
    Then for all sequences of Young diagrams $(\ya_k, \yb_k, \yc_k, \yd_k, \ye_k, \yf_k)$ indexed by $k > \tau_{\min}^{-1}$ with sizes given by \cref{eq:ceil_yd_sizes} and converging to $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ in the sense that \cref{eq:sextuple_convergence_rate} holds, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:seq_ud_sixj_upper_bound}
        \norm{\tightcurlysixj{ \ya_k & \yb_k & \yc_k \\ \yd_k &  \ye_k &  \yf_k}}_{2}^{2}
        \leq \exp\left(- \frac{k \tau_{\min}}{12} D_{*}^{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef} + O(\log k)\right),
    \end{equation}
    where $D_{*}^{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef} > 0$ is a positive constant which measures the distance from $(\ea, \eb, \ec, \ed, \ee, \ef)$ to the closed set of tetrahedral eigenvalues $(\tilde \ea, \tilde \eb, \tilde \ec, \tilde \ed, \tilde \ee, \tilde \ef)$ with matching traces (i.e., $\abs{\ea}=\abs{\tilde \ea}$, $\abs{\eb}=\abs{\tilde \eb}$, etc.):
    \begin{equation}
        D_{*}^{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef} =
        \min_{\substack{\tilde \ea \tilde \eb \tilde \ec\tilde\ed \tilde \ee \tilde \ef\\\mathrm{tetrahedral}\\\forall \ex : \abs{\tilde \ex}=\abs{\ex}}}
        \left(
        \norm{\nea - \tilde{\nea}}_{1}^{2} +
        \norm{\neb - \tilde{\neb}}_{1}^{2} +
        \norm{\nec - \tilde{\nec}}_{1}^{2} +
        \norm{\ned - \tilde{\ned}}_{1}^{2} +
        \norm{\nee - \tilde{\nee}}_{1}^{2} +
        \norm{\nef - \tilde{\nef}}_{1}^{2}
        \right)
        .
    \end{equation}
\end{cor}
\begin{proof}
    Using \cref{eq:tet_mean_value}, we have
    \begin{align}
        R_{\ya\yb\yd;\ye}\norm{\tightcurlysixj{ \ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd & \ye & \yf}}_{2}^2
        =
        \iiint \diff \mu(A)\diff \mu(B)\diff \mu(D)\TetGamma
    \end{align}
    where $R_{\ya\yb\yd;\ye}$ is a ratio of dimensions,
    \begin{equation}
        R_{\ya\yb\yd;\ye} 
        = h^n_\ya h^n_\yb h^n_\yd \frac{\dim V_\ye^n}{\dim(V_{\ya}^n \ot V_{\yb}^n \ot V_{\yd}^n)}
        =     \frac{\dim(V_{\ya}^{n})\dim(V_{\yb}^{n})\dim(V_{\yd}^{n})\dim(V_{\ye}^{n})}{\dim(V_{(a)}^{n^2})\dim(V_{(b)}^{n^2})\dim(V_{(d)}^{n^2})},
    \end{equation}
    and where the integral is taken over a triple of $n\times n$ trace one positive semidefinite matrices $A, B, D$.
    For each integrand, define a tetrahedral sextuple $(\tilde A,\tilde B,\tilde C,\tilde D,\tilde E,\tilde F)$ as follows
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:tilde_defns}
        \begin{alignedat}{3}
            &\tilde A \coloneqq \abs{\ea}A, \qquad
            &&\tilde B \coloneqq \abs{\eb}B, \qquad
            &&\tilde C \coloneqq \abs{\ea}A+\abs{\eb}B, \qquad \\
            &\tilde D \coloneqq \abs{\ed}D, \qquad
            &&\tilde E \coloneqq \abs{\ea}A+\abs{\eb}B+\abs{\ed}D, \qquad
            &&\tilde F \coloneqq \abs{\eb}B + \abs{\ed}D.
        \end{alignedat}
    \end{equation}
    Furthermore, let $(\tilde \ea, \tilde \eb, \tilde \ec, \tilde \ed, \tilde \ee, \tilde \ef)$ be the eigenvalues of $(\tilde A, \tilde B, \tilde C, \tilde D, \tilde E, \tilde F)$.
    In this notation, the integrand above is also equal to $\TetGammaTilde$.
    Moreover, by \cref{eq:tet_gamma_upper_bound} we have a bound on the integrand,
    \begin{equation}
        \abs{\TetGammaTilde} \leq p(k) \exp(-k I^{\ya\yb\yc\yd\ye\yf}_{\tilde \ea \tilde \eb\tilde \ec\tilde \ed\tilde \ee\tilde \ef}/6).
    \end{equation}
    %which implies
    %\begin{align}
    %    \norm{\tightcurlysixj{ \ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd &  \ye &  \yf}}_{2}^2
    %    &\leq R_{\ya,\yb,\yd;\ye}p(k)\exp(-k \inf_{\substack{\tilde \ea \tilde \eb \tilde \ec\tilde\ed \tilde \ee \tilde \ef\\\mathrm{tetrahedral}}} I^{\ya\yb\yc\yd\ye\yf}_{\tilde \ea \tilde \eb\tilde \ec\tilde \ed\tilde \ee\tilde \ef}/6)
    %\end{align}
    Now by construction, the eigenvalues $(\tilde \ea, \tilde \eb, \tilde \ec, \tilde \ed, \tilde \ee, \tilde \ef)$ are tetrahedral while by assumption, the eigenvalues $(\ea, \eb, \ec, \ed, \ee, \ef)$ are not tetrahedral (although they have matching traces due to \cref{eq:tilde_defns}).
    Therefore we conclude that
    \begin{equation}
        (\ea, \eb, \ec, \ed, \ee, \ef) \neq (\tilde \ea, \tilde \eb, \tilde \ec, \tilde \ed, \tilde \ee, \tilde \ef)
    \end{equation}
    and therefore $I_{\tilde \ea\tilde \eb\tilde \ec\tilde \ed\tilde \ee\tilde \ef}^{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef} > 0$.

    Our goal now will be to show that for any sequence of Young diagrams $(\ya_k, \yb_k, \yc_k, \yd_k, \ye_k, \yf_k)$ converging to the non-tetrahedral eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$, one can also guarantee that for sufficiently large $k$, the exponent $I_{\tilde \ea\tilde \eb\tilde \ec\tilde \ed\tilde \ee\tilde \ef}^{\ya_k\yb_k\yc_k\yd_k\ye_k\yf_k}$ above is also bounded from below by a positive constant.
    Let $\ex$ be a placeholder variable indexing $\ex \in \{\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef\}$.
    By Pinsker's inequality for the relative entropy, we obtain a sequence of lower bounds on $I^{\ya\yb\yc\yd\ye\yf}_{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}$:
    \begin{align}
        I^{\ya\yb\yc\yd\ye\yf}_{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}
        = \sum_{\ex} \abs{\ex} \kl{\nrm \yx}{\nrm \ex}
        \geq \frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ex} \abs{\ex} \norm{\nrm \yx - \nrm \ex}_{1}^{2}
        %&\geq \frac{1}{2} \max_{\ex} \abs{\ex} \norm{\nrm \yx - \nrm \ex}_{1}^{2}\\
        \geq \frac{\tau_{\min}}{2} \sum_{\ex} \norm{\nrm \yx - \nrm \ex}_{1}^{2}
        \geq \frac{\tau_{\min}}{2} \max_{\ex} \norm{\nrm \yx - \nrm \ex}_{1}^{2},
    \end{align}
    where $\tau_{\min}$ is the minimum trace over the eigenvalues (the lower indices).
    Applying the last bound to $I_{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}^{\ya_k\yb_k\yc_k\yd_k\ye_k\yf_k}$ yields
    \begin{equation}
        \max_{\ex}\norm{\nrm \yx_k - \nrm \ex}_{1} \leq \sqrt{2 \tau_{\min}^{-1} I_{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}^{\ya_k\yb_k\yc_k\yd_k\ye_k\yf_k}}
    \end{equation}
    whereas applying the second to last bound to $I_{\tilde \ea\tilde \eb \tilde \ec\tilde \ed\tilde \ee\tilde \ef}^{\ya_k\yb_k\yc_k\yd_k\ye_k\yf_k}$ (noting that $\tau_{\min}$ is also the minimum trace of $(\tilde \ea,\tilde \eb, \tilde \ec, \tilde \ed, \tilde \ee, \tilde \ef)$) yields
    \begin{equation}
        \sum_{\ex} \norm{\nrm \yx_k - \nrm {\tilde \ex}}_{1}^2 \leq 2 \tau_{\min}^{-1}I^{\ya_k\yb_k\yc_k\yd_k\ye_k\yf_k}_{\tilde \ea \tilde \eb\tilde \ec\tilde \ed\tilde \ee\tilde \ef}.
    \end{equation}
    Furthermore, by the reverse triangle inequality we have
    \begin{align}
        \sum_{\ex} \norm{\nrm \yx_k - \nrm {\tilde \ex}}_{1}^2
        \geq
        \sum_{\ex}\left(\norm{\nrm \yx_k - \nrm \ex}_{1}
        -\norm{\nrm \ex - \nrm {\tilde \ex}}_{1}\right)^2
        \geq
        \sum_{\ex}\norm{\nrm \ex - \nrm {\tilde \ex}}_{1}^2 -2\norm{\nrm \yx_k - \nrm \ex}_{1}\norm{\nrm \ex - \nrm {\tilde \ex}}_{1}.
    \end{align}
    Also by the definition of $D_{*}^{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}$ we have
    \begin{equation}
        D_{*}^{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef} \leq \sum_{\ex}\norm{\nrm \ex - \nrm {\tilde \ex}}_{1}^2.
    \end{equation}
    Since $\nrm \ex$ and $\nrm {\tilde \ex}$ are both probability distributions we also have $\norm{\nrm \ex - \nrm {\tilde \ex}}_{1} \leq 2$.
    Also, $6 \max_{\ex}\norm{\nrm \yx_k - \nrm \ex}_{1} \geq \sum_{\ex}\norm{\nrm \yx_k - \nrm \ex}_{1}$ as there are six summands.
    Combining all these bounds together yields
    %\begin{align}
    %    I^{\ya_k\yb_k\yc_k\yd_k\ye_k\yf_k}_{\tilde \ea \tilde \eb\tilde \ec\tilde \ed\tilde \ee\tilde \ef}
    %    \geq
    %    \frac{\tau_{\min}}{2} \sum_{\ex} \norm{\nrm \yx_k - \nrm {\tilde \ex}}_{1}^2
    %    &\geq \frac{\tau_{\min}}{2}\left(D_{*}^{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}
    %    -24\sqrt{2 \tau_{\min}^{-1} I_{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}^{\ya_k\yb_k\yc_k\yd_k\ye_k\yf_k}}\right),
    %\end{align}
    %and therefore,
    \begin{equation}
        \norm{\tightcurlysixj{ \ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd & \ye & \yf}}_{2}^2
        \leq  \exp\left(-k\left\{\frac{\tau_{\min} D_{*}^{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}}{12}
        -2\sqrt{2 \tau_{\min} I_{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}^{\ya_k\yb_k\yc_k\yd_k\ye_k\yf_k}} - \frac{1}{k}\log(R_{\ya_k,\yb_k,\yd_k;\ye_k}^{-1} p(k))\right\}\right).
    \end{equation}
    By assumption, the Young diagrams $(\ya_k, \yb_k, \yc_k, \yd_k, \ye_k, \yf_k)$ converge in normalization to the eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ in the sense that $I_{\ea\eb\ec\ed\ee\ef}^{\ya_k\yb_k\yc_k\yd_k\ye_k\yf_k}$ tends to zero as $k\to \infty$ at a rate given by \cref{eq:sextuple_convergence_rate}.
    Moreover, $R_{\ya_k,\yb_k,\yd_k;\ye_k}^{-1}$ can be upper bounded by a polynomial in $k$ and $p(k)$ is a polynomial in $k$ so \cref{eq:seq_ud_sixj_upper_bound} holds as claimed.
\end{proof}

\clearpage
\subsection{Drafting: Reformulating the main results}
TC: Here I'm reformulating everything with two objectives: (i) to invoke eigenvalue estimation as late as possible, and (ii) to \textit{discover} the ``$\Gamma$'' quantities I had defined in a more organic way.
For example, the tri-gamma quantitity remerges as a summand in the binomial expansion theorem.

\begin{defn}
    Let $(\pi_{\yx}, V_{\yx}^n)$ be an irreducible representation of $\U(n)$ labelled by the Young diagram $\yx$.
    Given an $n \times n$ matrix $X$, viewed as a linear operator on the defining representation $V_{(1)}^{n} \simeq \mathbb C^{n}$, we define $\pi_{\yx}(X)$ by the formula:
    \begin{equation*}
        \pi_{\yx}(X) \coloneqq \frac{\Tr_{W_{\yx}}[\iota_{\yx}^{\dagger} X^{\ot \abs{\yx}} \iota_{\yx}]}{\dim(W_{\yx})}
    \end{equation*}
    where $\iota_{\yx} : W_{\yx} \ot V_{\yx}^{n} \to V_{(1)}^{\ot \abs{\yx}}$ is the equivariant isometry for the $\yx$-isotypic subspace of $V_{(1)}^{\ot \abs{\yx}}$.
    This definition ensures that $X^{\ot k} \simeq \bigoplus_{\yx \vdash k} \pi_{\yx}(X)^{\oplus W_{\yx}}$ holds for any positive integer $k$.
    When $X$ is a unitary, $\pi_{\yx}(X)$ is the representation of $X$ on $V_{\yx}^{n}$, so this abuse of notation is warranted.
    If $X \geq 0$ is positive semidefinite, then $\pi_{\yx}(X) \geq 0$ is positive semidefinite also.
    If $\yx = (1)$, then $\pi_{(1)}(X) = X$ as $\dim(W_{(1)}) = 1$.

    Note also that the matrix coefficients of $\pi_{\yx}(X)$ are homogeneous degree $\abs{\lambda}$ polynomials in the matrix coefficients of $X$ meaning for any scalar $s \in \mathbb C$,
    \begin{equation}
        \pi_{\yx}(sX) = s^{\abs{\yx}} \pi_{\yx}(X).
    \end{equation}
    Additionally, if $X,Y$ are two matrices, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \pi_{\yx}(XY) = \pi_{\yx}(X)\pi_{\yx}(Y). 
    \end{equation}
    The \textit{character} of $X$ satisfies
    \begin{equation*}
        \chi_{\yx}(X) \coloneqq \Tr(\pi_{\yx}(X)) = \frac{\Tr[P_{\yx} X^{\ot \abs{\yx}}]}{\dim(W_{\yx})} = s_{\yx}(\eig(X)),
    \end{equation*}
    where $s_{\yx}(\eig(X))$ is the corresponding Schur-polynomial evaluated on the eigenvalues of $X$.
    For fixed positive integer $k$, we also define the \textit{Plancherel distribution} $\{\mu_\yx | \yx \vdash k\}$ over Young diagrams of size $k$ according to
    \begin{equation}
        \mu_\yx \coloneqq \frac{(\dim W_\yx)^2}{\abs{\yx}!}.
    \end{equation}
    Moreover, we define the rescaling of $\pi_{\yx}(X)$ to be
    \begin{equation*}
        \tilde \pi_{\yx}(X) \coloneqq \dim(W_{\yx}) \pi_{\yx}(X) = \Tr_{W_{\yx}}[\iota_{\yx}^{\dagger} X^{\ot \abs{\yx}} \iota_{\yx}],
    \end{equation*}
    and the corresponding rescaled character is
    \begin{equation*}
        \tilde \chi_\yx(X) \coloneqq \Tr[\tilde \pi_{\yx}(X)] = \Tr[P_{\yx} X^{\ot \abs{\yx}}] = \dim(W_{\yx})s_{\yx}(\eig(X)),
    \end{equation*}
    where $P_{\yx} \coloneqq \iota_{\yx}^{\dagger} \iota_{\yx}$ is the orthogonal projection operator onto the $\yx$-isotypic subspace.

    These rescaled characters emerge as coefficients in the following decomposition associated to an integral over Haar measure over $\U(n)$,
    \begin{equation}
        \int_{\U(n)} \diff U (U X U^{-1})^{\ot k} = \sum_{\lambda \vdash k} \tilde \chi_{\yx}(X)   \frac{P_{\yx}}{\Tr(P_{\yx})}.
    \end{equation}
    This normalization scheme means that $\tilde \pi_{\yx}(X)$ is \textit{not} a representation, while the character $\tilde \chi_{\yx}(X)$ satisfies
    \begin{equation}
        \sum_{\yx \vdash k} \tilde \chi_{\yx}(X) = \sum_{\yx \vdash k} \Tr[X^{\ot k} P_{\yx}] = \Tr[X^{\ot k}] = \Tr[X]^k.
    \end{equation}
    Therefore, whenever $X$ is positive semidefinite and trace one (i.e., when $X$ is a density matrix), we have that the map $\yx \mapsto \tilde \chi_{\yx}(X)$ is a probability distribution when restricted to the set of Young diagrams with a fixed number $k$ of boxes:
    \begin{equation}
        X\geq 0 \implies \tilde \chi_{\yx}(X) \geq 0, \qquad \text{and} \qquad \Tr(X) = 1 \implies \sum_{\yx \vdash k} \tilde \chi_{\yx}(X) = 1.
    \end{equation}
    As the (rescaled) character $\chi_{\yx}(X)$ depends only on the eigenvalues $\eig(X)$ of $X$, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \chi_{\yx}(X) = \chi_{\yx}(\diag(\eig(X)).
    \end{equation}
    Given a tuple of non-negative sorted eigenvalues $\ex = (\ex_1 \geq \cdots \geq \ex_n \geq 0)$ summing to one, $\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ex_i = 1$, the distribution $\yx \mapsto \tilde \chi_{\yx}(\diag(\ex))$ over $\yx \vdash k$ is denoted $\mathrm{SW}^{k}(\ex)$ and called the \textit{Schur-Weyl} distribution in \cite{odonnell2016efficient}.
\end{defn}
\begin{prop}[Eigenvalue estimation]
    Let $X \geq 0$ be a positive semidefinite $n\times n$ matrix with eigenvalues $\ex = \eig(X)$.
    Then the Schur-Weyl distribution $\tilde \chi_{\yx}(\nrm X) = \Tr(P_{\yx} \nrm X^{\ot k})$ over $\yx \vdash k$ satisfies
    \begin{equation}
        \tilde \chi_{\yx}(\nrm X) \leq (k+1)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}} \exp(-k \kl{\nrm \yx}{\nrm \ex})
    \end{equation}
    and
    \begin{equation}
        \tilde \chi_{\yx}(\nrm X) \geq (k+n)^{-\frac{(n+2)(n-1)}{2}} \exp(-k \kl{\nrm \yx}{\nrm \ex})
    \end{equation}
    where $\kl{\nrm \yx}{\nrm \ex}$ denotes the Kullback-Leibler divergence of the normalized Young diagram $\nrm \yx$ from the normalized eigenvalue $\nrm \ex$.
\end{prop}
%\begin{rem}
%    We will use the eigenvalue estimation bounds in two essentially related ways.
%    Let $N^{k}_{\varepsilon}(\nrm \ex)$ denote the set of Young diagrams $\yx$ of size $k$ which are close to the normalized eigenvalues $\nrm \ex$:
%    \begin{equation}
%        N^{k}_{\varepsilon}(\nrm \ex) \coloneqq \{ \yx \vdash k \mid \kl{\nrm \yx}{\nrm \ex} < \varepsilon \}.
%    \end{equation}
%    Then we have a lower bound,
%    \begin{equation}
%        \yx \in N^{k}_{\varepsilon}(\nrm \ex) \implies \tilde \chi_{\yx}(\nrm X) \geq (k+n)^{-\frac{(n+2)(n-1)}{2}} \exp(- k \varepsilon).
%    \end{equation}
%\end{rem}
\begin{prop}[Eigenvalue separation]
    Let $(\ea, \eb)$ be eigenvalues of positive semidefinite $n\times n$ matrices $(A,B)$ and let $s = \norm{\nrm \ea - \nrm \eb}_1$ be the separation between the eigenvalues of $\nrm A$ and $\nrm B$.
    For any positive powers $p,q > 0$, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \tilde \chi_{\yx}(\nrm A)^p\tilde \chi_{\yx}(\nrm B)^{q} \leq (\dim V_{\yx}^{n})^{p+q} \exp\left(-\abs{\yx} s^2\frac{pq}{2(p+q)}\right).
    \end{equation}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    The proof follows from eigenvalue estimation for a Young diagram $\yx$ which yields
    \begin{equation}
        \tilde \chi_{\yx}(\nrm A)^p \leq (\dim V_{\yx}^{n})^p \exp( - \abs{\yx} p\kl{\nrm \yx}{\nrm \ea}).
    \end{equation}
    Furthermore, Pinsker's inequality yields
    \begin{align}
        p\kl{\nrm \yx}{\nrm \ea} + q\kl{\nrm \yx}{\nrm \eb}
        \geq \frac{p}{2} \norm{\nrm \yx - \nrm \ea}_1^{2} + \frac{q}{2}\norm{\nrm \yx - \nrm \eb}_1^{2}
        \geq \frac{pq}{2(p+q)} \norm{\nrm \ea - \nrm \eb}_1^{2},
    \end{align}
    where the last inequality arises because the weighted sum of squared norms is minimized when $(p+q)\nrm \yx = p \nrm \ea + q \nrm \eb$.
\end{proof}
It is well-known that there is a multiplicity-free decomposition of the representation $V_{(k)}^{n^2}$ of $\U(n^2)$ under the restriction to the subgroup $\U(n) \times \U(n)$ known as \citeauthor{howe1987gl}-duality \cite{howe1987gl}:
\begin{equation}
    V_{(k)}^{n^2} \cong \bigoplus_{\yx \vdash k} V_{\yx}^{n} \ot V_{\yx}^{n}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, this provides a probability distribution over Young diagrams $\yx \vdash k$, which we refer to as the \textit{Howe distribution} with probabilities given by
\begin{equation}
    h^n_\yx \coloneqq \frac{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})^2}{\dim(V_{(k)}^{n^2})}.
\end{equation}
The Howe distribution also appears when evaluating $\tilde \pi_{\yx}(X)$ for $X$ sampled randomly according to the so-called Hilbert-Schmidt measure \cite{zyczkowski2001induced}.
\begin{prop}
    Let $X \geq 0$ be a random positive semidefinite $n \times n$ matrix such that $\Tr(X) = 1$ sampled according to the Hilbert-Schmidt measure.
    Then for $\yx \vdash k$,
    \begin{align}
        \mathbb E_{X \sim 1} \tilde \pi_{\yx}(X) = h^n_\yx \frac{\id_{V_{\yx}^{n}}}{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})}.
    \end{align}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    Given a measure $\mu(X)$ that is invariant under conjugation by $\U(n)$, the operator $\mathbb E_{X} [X^{\ot k}]$ is necessarily $\U(n)$ equivariant and thus decomposes as 
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb E_{X\sim 1} [X^{\ot k}] \coloneqq \int \diff \mu (X) X^{\ot k} = \sum_{\yx \vdash k} h_{\yx} \frac{P_{\yx}}{\Tr(P_{\yx})},
    \end{equation}
    for some proportionality constants $h_{\yx}$.
    For the Hilbert Schmidt measure, which is equivalent to the pushforward measure of the Haar measure induced by $\U(n^2)$ on $\mathbb C^{n^2}$ under the partial trace to operators on $\mathbb C^{n}$ \cite{zyczkowski2001induced}, the proportionality constant given by (see also \cite{walter2014multipartite}) the Howe distribution parameter $h^n_\yx$.
    Therefore,
    \begin{align}
        \mathbb E_{X} [\tilde \pi_{\yx}(X)]
        = \Tr_{W_{\yx}}[\iota_{\yx}^{\dagger} \mathbb E_{X} [X^{\ot \abs{\yx}}] \iota_{\yx}]
        = h^n_\yx \frac{\Tr_{W_{\yx}}[\iota_{\yx}^{\dagger} P_{\yx} \iota_{\yx}]}{\Tr(P_{\yx})}
        = h^n_\yx \frac{\Tr_{W_{\yx}}[\id_{W_{\yx}} \ot \id_{V_{\yx}^{n}}]}{\Tr(P_{\yx})}
        = h^n_\yx \frac{\id_{V_{\yx}^{n}}}{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})}.
    \end{align}
\end{proof}
\begin{thm}[Binomial theorem] 
    Let $A,B$ be $n \times n$ matrices.
    Then
    \begin{equation}
        \tilde \pi_\yc(A+B) = \sum_{\ya,\yb} \frac{\abs{\yc}!}{\abs{\ya}!\abs{\yb}!} \Phi^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb} [\tilde \pi_{\ya}(A) \ot \tilde \pi_{\yb}(B)]
    \end{equation}
    where $\Phi^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}$ is the linear superoperator sending operators on $V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n}$ to operators on $V_{\yc}^{n}$ according to the formula
    \begin{equation}
        \Phi^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}[\cdot] = \Tr_{C^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}} \left[ (\iota^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb})^{\dagger} \cdot (\iota^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}) \right],
    \end{equation}
    where $\iota^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb} : C^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb} \ot V_{\yc}^{n} \to V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n}$ is the equivariant isometry for the $\yc$-isotypic subspace of $V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n}$.
    If $C^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}$ is zero-dimensional, then $\iota^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}$ and $\Phi^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}$ are taken to be zero maps.
\end{thm}
\begin{proof}
    By definition, we have $\tilde \pi_{\yc}(A+B) = \Tr_{W_{\yc}}[\iota_{\yc}^{\dagger} (A+B)^{\ot \abs{\yc}} \iota_{\yc}]$. 
    Note that we can expand $(A+B)^{\ot k}$ using a non-commutative binomial expansion:
    \begin{equation}
        (A+B)^{\ot k} = \sum_{\ell=0}^{k} \binom{k}{\ell}  \frac{1}{k!} \sum_{\sigma \in S_{k}} \sigma^{-1} (A^{\ot \ell} \otimes B^{\ot (k - \ell)})\sigma
    \end{equation}
    were $\sigma \in S_{k}$ acts on $(\mathbb C^{n})^{\ot k}$ by permutation of tensor factors.
    However, as the superoperator $\Tr_{W_{\yc}}[\iota_{\yc}^{\dagger} \cdot \iota_{\yc}]$ is manifestly $S_{\abs{\yc}}$-invariant (due to the partial trace over the representation space $W_{\yc}$ of $S_{\abs{\yc}}$), the normalized sum over $\sigma \in S_{\abs{\yc}}$ dissappears and one obtains
    \begin{equation}
        \tilde \pi_{\yc}(A+B) = \sum_{\ell=0}^{k} \binom{k}{\ell} \Tr_{W_{\yc}}[\iota_{\yc}^{\dagger} (A^{\ot \ell} \otimes B^{\ot (k - \ell)}) \iota_{\yc}].
    \end{equation}
    Next decompose $A^{\ot \ell}$ (resp. $B^{\ot (k - \ell)}$) into isotypic components indexed by $\ya \vdash \ell$ (resp. $\yb \vdash k - \ell$) to obtain
    \begin{equation}
        A^{\ot \ell} \otimes B^{\ot (k - \ell)} = \sum_{\substack{\ya\vdash \ell\\\yb\vdash k -\ell}} (\iota_{\ya} \ot \iota_{\yb}) [(\id_{W_{\ya}} \ot \pi_{\ya}(A))\ot(\id_{W_{\yb}} \ot \pi_{\yb}(B))] (\iota_{\ya} \ot \iota_{\yb})^{\dagger}.
    \end{equation}
    Combining these results and noting that the $S_{\abs{\yc}}$-representation space $W_{\yc}$ recomposes as $W_{\yc} \simeq \bigoplus_{\ya,\yb} C^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb} \ot W_{\ya} \ot W_{\yb}$ under the restriction to the subgroup $S_{\abs{\ya}} \times S_{\abs{\yb}}$ acting on the first $\ell$ and last $k-\ell$ factors respectively produces
    \begin{align}
        \tilde \pi_{\yc}(A+B) 
        &= \sum_{\ell=0}^{k} \binom{k}{\ell} \sum_{\substack{\ya\vdash \ell\\\yb\vdash k -\ell}} \Tr_{W_{\yc}}[\iota_{\yc}^{\dagger} (\iota_{\alpha} \ot \iota_{\yb}) [(\id_{W_{\ya}} \ot \pi_{\ya}(A))\ot(\id_{W_{\yb}} \ot \pi_{\yb}(B))] (\iota_{\alpha} \ot \iota_{\yb})^{\dagger} \iota_{\yc}] \\
        &= \sum_{\ell=0}^{k} \binom{k}{\ell} \sum_{\substack{\ya\vdash \ell\\\yb\vdash k -\ell}} \dim(W_\ya)\dim(W_\yb) \Tr_{C^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}} [ (\iota^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb})^{\dagger} \pi_\ya(A) \ot \pi_\yb(B) (\iota^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb})], \\
        &= \sum_{\alpha,\beta} \frac{\abs{\yc}!}{\abs{\ya}!\abs{\yb}!} \Tr_{C^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}} [ (\iota^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb})^{\dagger} \tilde \pi_\ya(A) \ot \tilde \pi_\yb(B) (\iota^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb})],
    \end{align}
    where the final line follows as the sum in both cases is over all $\ya,\yb$ such that $\abs{\yc} = \abs{\ya}+\abs{\yb}$ as the multiplicity space $C^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}$ is zero whenever $\abs{\yc} \neq \abs{\ya}+\abs{\yb}$.
\end{proof}
\begin{cor}
    For $x > 0 $, let $X \sim x$ denote a random positive semidefinite matrix sampled such that its trace is fixed, $\Tr(X) = x$, and its normalization, $\nrm X \coloneqq X/\Tr(X)$, is distributed according to the Hilbert-Schmidt measure.
    Fix $a,b,c \in \mathbb R_{\geq 0}$ such that $c=a+b$.
    Then
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb E_{\substack{A \sim a \\ B \sim b}} \tilde \pi_{\yc}(\nrm{A+B}) 
        = \id_{V_{\yc}^n} \sum_{\ya,\yb} \frac{\abs{\yc}!}{\abs{\ya}!\abs{\yb}!} 
        \frac{a^{\abs{\ya}}b^{\abs{\yb}}}{c^{\abs{\yc}}}
        \frac{c^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}h_{\ya}h_{\yb}}{\dim(V_{\ya}^n \ot V_{\yb}^n)} 
    \end{equation}
\end{cor}

    Let $\nu_{\ec}$ be the probability distribution over $\{\yc \vdash c\}$ given by
    \begin{equation}
        \nu_{\ec}(\yc) \coloneqq \iint \diff W \Tr[P_\yc (\nrm{W\cdot \ec})^{\ot c}] = \Tr[P_\yc (\nrm{\ec})^{\ot c}] = \tilde \chi_{\yc}(\nrm{\ec}).
    \end{equation}
    Moreover, let $\mu_{\ea,\eb}$ be the probability distribution over the same space given by
    \begin{equation}
        \mu_{\ea,\eb}(\yc) \coloneqq \iint \! \diff U\diff V \Tr[P_\yc (\nrm{U\cdot \ea + V \cdot \eb})^{\ot c}].
    \end{equation}
    Performing the integral over $U,V \in U(n)$ (and assuming $\etr{\ec}=\etr{\ea}+\etr{\eb}$) yields the formula:
    \begin{align}
        \mu_{\ea,\eb}(\yc) 
        &= \sum_{a+b=c}\frac{c!}{a!b!}\frac{\etr{\ea}^a\etr{\eb}^b}{\etr{\ec}^c} \sum_{\ya\vdash a,\yb \vdash b} \iint \! \diff U\diff V \Tr[P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \tilde \pi_\ya(\nrm {U\cdot \ea}) \ot \tilde \pi_\yb(\nrm {V \cdot \eb})], \\
        &= \sum_{a+b=c}\frac{c!}{a!b!}\frac{\etr{\ea}^a\etr{\eb}^b}{\etr{\ec}^c} \sum_{\ya\vdash a,\yb \vdash b} \tilde \chi_{\ya}(\nrm \ea)\tilde\chi_{\yb}(\nrm \eb)\frac{\dim (V_{\yc}^n \ot C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb})}{\dim (V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n})}.
    \end{align}
    %where $T_{\ya,\yb}^{\yc;n}$ is a probability distribution over $\{\yc \vdash c\}$ conditioned on fixed $\ya,\yb$ given by the decomposition of the tensor $V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n}$ into isotypic components:
    %\begin{equation}
    %    T_{\ya,\yb}^{\yc;n} \coloneqq \frac{\dim (V_{\yc}^n \ot C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb})}{\dim (V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n})}
    %\end{equation}
    %Also define for $\ya \vdash a, \yb \vdash b$, and $\yc \vdash c = a+b$ the joint probability:
    %\begin{equation}
    %    \mu_{\ea,\eb}(\ya,\yb,\yc) = \frac{c!}{a!b!}\frac{\etr{\ea}^a\etr{\eb}^b}{\etr{\ec}^c} \tilde \chi_{\ya}(\nrm \ea)\tilde\chi_{\yb}(\nrm \eb) \frac{\dim (V_{\yc}^n \ot C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb})}{\dim (V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n})}
    %\end{equation}
    %One can then consider the quantity
    %\begin{equation}
    %    F_{\ea,\eb;\ec}^{(c),s_1,s_2} = \sum_{\yc\vdash c}\tilde \chi_{\yc}(\nrm{\ec})^{s_1}
    %    \left(\sum_{a+b=c}\frac{c!}{a!b!}\frac{\etr{\ea}^a\etr{\eb}^b}{\etr{\ec}^c} \sum_{\ya\vdash a,\yb \vdash b} \tilde \chi_{\ya}(\nrm \ea)\tilde\chi_{\yb}(\nrm \eb) \frac{\dim (V_{\yc}^n \ot C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb})}{\dim (V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n})}\right)^{s_2}
    %\end{equation}
    Now for \textit{any} $(A,B)$ with eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb)$ such that $C = A+B$ has eigenvalues $\ec$, we have the identity
    \begin{equation}
        \nu_{\ec}(\yc) = \sum_{a+b=c}\frac{c!}{a!b!}\frac{\etr{\ea}^a\etr{\eb}^b}{\etr{\ec}^c} \sum_{\ya\vdash a,\yb \vdash b} \Tr[P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \tilde \pi_\ya(\nrm A) \ot \tilde \pi_\yb(\nrm B)].
    \end{equation}
    %Also define the joint probability:
    %\begin{equation}
    %    \nu_{\ec}^{A,B}(\ya,\yb,\yc) = \frac{c!}{a!b!}\frac{\etr{\ea}^a\etr{\eb}^b}{\etr{\ec}^c}\Tr[P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \tilde \pi_\ya(\nrm A) \ot \tilde \pi_\yb(\nrm B)].
    %\end{equation}
    Note that $\nu_{\ec}$ and $\mu_{\ea,\eb}$ have a very similar form under the assumption that $(\ea,\eb,\ec)$ are trigonal.
    Already, one observes that
    \begin{equation}
        \mu_{\ea,\eb}(\yc) \leq \max_{\ec} \nu_{\ec}(\yc)
    \end{equation}
    where the maximum is taken over all eigenvalues $\ec$ such that $(\ea,\eb,\yc)$ are compatible.

    To relate these two quantities more closely, we use that $P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}$ is a projection operator and that $A$ and $B$ are both positive semidefinite to obtain:
    \begin{equation}
        \Tr[P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \tilde \pi_\ya(\nrm A) \ot \tilde \pi_\yb(\nrm B)] \leq \Tr[\tilde \pi_\ya(\nrm A)]\Tr[\tilde \pi_\yb(\nrm B)] = \tilde \chi_{\ya}(\nrm \ea)\tilde\chi_{\yb}(\nrm \eb).
    \end{equation}
    %Therefore we obtain
    %\begin{equation}
    %    \frac{\dim (V_{\yc}^n \ot C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb})}{\dim (V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n})} \nu_{\ec}(\ya,\yb,\yc) \leq \mu_{\ea,\eb}(\ya,\yb,\yc)
    %\end{equation}
    Now consider the bound $\dim V_\yx^n \leq (\abs{\yx}+1)^{n(n-1)/2}$.
    Since $\abs{\ya} = a, \abs{\yb} = b$ and $a+b=c$, we have 
    \begin{equation}
        (a+1)(b+1) = ab + a+b + 1 \leq \frac{c^2}{4}+c+1
    \end{equation}
    and therefore whenever $c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \neq 0$, we obtain a $(\ya,\yb)$-independent inequality:
    \begin{equation}
        \frac{\dim (V_{\yc}^n \ot C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb})}{\dim (V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n})} \geq \frac{\dim V_{\yc}^n}{\dim (V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n})} \geq \frac{\dim V_{\yc}^n}{p_n(c)},
    \end{equation}
    where $p_n(c) \coloneqq (c^2/4+c+1)^{n(n-1)/2}$.
    Altogether we obtain the inequality:
    \begin{equation}
        \mu_{\ea,\eb}(\yc) \geq \frac{\dim V_{\yc}^n}{p_n(c)}\nu_{\ec}(\yc).
    \end{equation}
    which holds for all $\yc \vdash c$ independently of the decomposition of $C = A+B$.
        
    The fidelity between $\mu_{\ea,\eb}$ and $\nu_{\ec}$ over $\{\yc \vdash c\}$ is given by
    \begin{equation}
        F_{\ea,\eb;\ec}^{(c)} \coloneqq \left(\sum_{\yc\vdash c} \sqrt{\mu_{\ea,\eb}(\yc)\nu_{\ec}(\yc)}\right)^{2},
    \end{equation}
    or alternatively, the inner product between $\mu_{\ea,\eb}$ and $\nu_{\ec}$ is
    \begin{align}
        W_{\ea,\eb;\ec}^{(c)} 
        &\coloneqq \sum_{\yc\vdash c} \mu_{\ea,\eb}(\yc)\nu_{\ec}(\yc),\\
        &= \sum_{a+b=c}\frac{c!}{a!b!}\frac{\etr{\ea}^a\etr{\eb}^b}{\etr{\ec}^c} \sum_{\substack{\ya\vdash a,\yb \vdash b \\ \yc\vdash c}} \tilde \chi_{\ya}(\nrm \ea)\tilde\chi_{\yb}(\nrm \eb)\tilde\chi_{\yc}(\nrm \ec)\frac{\dim (V_{\yc}^n \ot C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb})}{\dim (V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n})}.
    \end{align}
    Our inequality between $\mu_{\ea,\eb}$ and $\nu_{\ec}$ implies the lower bounds
    \begin{equation}
        F_{\ea,\eb;\ec}^{(c)} \geq \frac{1}{p_n(c)}, \qquad W_{\ea,\eb;\ec}^{(c)} \geq \frac{1}{(c+1)^{n-1}p_n(c)}
    \end{equation}
    as there at most $\abs{\{\yc \vdash c\}} \leq (c+1)^{n-1}$ summands.
    To obtain a complementary upper bound, let
    \begin{equation}
        \Delta_{\ea,\eb;\ec} \coloneqq \min_{U,V}\{\norm{\eig(\nrm{U\cdot \ea + V\cdot \eb})-\nrm \ec}_1\}.
    \end{equation}
    Then, by the eigenvalue separation lemma, we obtain a $(U,V)$-independent upper bound:
    \begin{align}
        \Tr[P_\yc (\nrm{U\cdot \ea + V \cdot \eb})^{\ot c}]\Tr[P_\yc (\nrm{\ec})^{\ot c}]
        &\leq (c+1)^{n(n-1)} \exp(-\frac{c}{4} \Delta_{\ea,\eb;\yc}^2),
    \end{align}
    Therefore, for $F_{\ea,\eb;\ec}^{(c)}$ we have
    \begin{equation}
        F_{\ea,\eb;\ec}^{(c)} \leq (c+1)^{n^2+n-2}\exp(-\frac{c}{4} \Delta_{\ea,\eb;\ec}^2),
    \end{equation}
    while, for $W_{\ea,\eb;\ec}^{(c)}$ we have
    \begin{equation}
        W_{\ea,\eb;\ec}^{(c)} \leq (c+1)^{n^2-1} \exp(-\frac{c}{4} \Delta_{\ea,\eb;\ec}^2).
    \end{equation}
    %Therefore, if $r = \rank(\ec)$ then $\card{\{\yc \vdash_r c\}} \leq (c+1)^{r-1}$ and therefore we obtain:
    %\begin{equation}
    %    \Delta_{\ea,\eb;\yc} \leq \sqrt{2(3n(n-1)+r-1)\frac{\ln (c+1)}{c}}.
    %\end{equation}
    \begin{thm}
        Fix a positive integer $k$.
        If $(\ea,\eb,\ec)$ are the eigenvalues of positive semidefinite $n\times n$ matrices $(A,B,C)$ satisfying $A+B=C$, then for every positive integer $k$,
        \begin{equation}
            W_{\ea,\eb;\ec}^{k} \geq \frac{1}{(k+1)^{n-1}p_n(k)}.
        \end{equation}
        Conversely, if $(\ea,\eb,\ec)$ are not the eigenvalues of any such matrices and $\Delta_{\ea,\eb;\yc} > \varepsilon$, then the above inequality is violated for all $k$ such that
        \begin{equation}
            \frac{k}{\ln ((k+1)^{n^2+n-2}p_n(k))}>\frac{4}{\varepsilon^2}.
        \end{equation}
    \end{thm}
\hrule
    For the specific case of $n =2$, and $\yx = (\yx_1, \yx_2)$ with $\abs{\yx} = \yx_1 + \yx_2 = k$, we have
    \begin{equation}
        d_\yx \coloneqq \dim V_{(\yx_1,\yx_2)}^{2} = \yx_1 - \yx_2 + 1 \leq k+1.
    \end{equation}
    \begin{equation}
        \tilde \chi_{\yx}(x) = \Tr[P_{(\yx_1,\yx_2)} \diag(x_1,x_2)^{\ot k}] = d_{\yx}\frac{k!}{(\yx_1+1)!\yx_2!} (x_1x_2)^{\yx_2} \frac{x_1^{d_\yx} - x_2^{d_\yx}}{x_1 - x_2}.
    \end{equation}
    Therefore, 
    \begin{equation}
        \mu_{a,b}(\yx) = d_{\yx}\frac{k!}{(c_1+c_2)^k} \sum_{j=0}^{k} \sum_{\ya\vdash j,\yb \vdash k-j} \frac{(a_1a_2)^{\ya_2}}{(\ya_1+1)!\ya_2!} \frac{a_1^{d_\ya} - a_2^{d_\ya}}{a_1 - a_2}\frac{ (b_1b_2)^{\yb_2}}{(\yb_1+1)!\yb_2!} \frac{b_1^{d_\yb} - b_2^{d_\yb}}{b_1 - b_2}c^{\yx}_{\ya\yb}
    \end{equation}
    \begin{equation}
        \nu_{c}(\yx) = d_{\yx}\frac{k!}{(c_1+c_2)^k}\frac{(c_1c_2)^{\yx_2}}{(\yx_1+1)!\yx_2!}  \frac{c_1^{d_\yx} - c_2^{d_\yx}}{c_1 - c_2}.
    \end{equation}
    Which means the inequality $\mu_{a,b}(\yx) \geq \frac{d_{\yx}}{k^2/4+k+1} \nu_{c}(\yx)$ simplifies to
    \begin{equation}
        \left(\sum_{j=0}^{k} \sum_{\ya\vdash j,\yb \vdash k-j} \frac{(a_1a_2)^{\ya_2}}{(\ya_1+1)!\ya_2!} \frac{a_1^{d_\ya} - a_2^{d_\ya}}{a_1 - a_2}\frac{ (b_1b_2)^{\yb_2}}{(\yb_1+1)!\yb_2!} \frac{b_1^{d_\yb} - b_2^{d_\yb}}{b_1 - b_2}c^{\yx}_{\ya\yb}\right) 
        \geq \frac{d_{\yx}}{k^2/4+k+1}
        \left(\frac{(c_1c_2)^{\yx_2}}{(\yx_1+1)!\yx_2!}  \frac{c_1^{d_\yx} - c_2^{d_\yx}}{c_1 - c_2}\right)
    \end{equation}
    Letting $\delta_\ya = \ya_1 - \ya_2$, etc. and noting that $c^{\yx}_{\ya\yb} \in \{0,1\}$ with $c^{\yx}_{\ya\yb} = 1$ iff $\abs{\delta_{\ya}-\delta_{\yb}} \leq \delta_{\yx} \leq \delta_{\ya}+\delta_{\yb}$, we have

    If $c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} > 0$, then let $\nu^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb} : V_{\yc}^{n} \to V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n}$ and $\omega^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} : W_{\ya} \ot W_{\yb} \to W_{\yc}$ be a Schur-Weyl-dual pair of equivariant isometries.
    Then we have an operator on $V_{\yc}^{n}$ of the form
    \begin{equation}
        (\nu^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb})^{\dagger}(\tilde \pi_{\ya}(A) \ot \tilde \pi_{\yb}(B))(\nu^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}) = \Tr_{W_{\yc}}[(\phi^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb})^{\dagger} (A^{\ot a} \ot B^{\ot b}) (\phi^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb})]
    \end{equation}
    where $\phi^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb} = (\iota_{\ya} \ot \iota_{\yb})(\omega^{\yc;\dagger}_{\ya\yb} \ot \nu^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb})$.
    By $S_{c}$-invariance (due to the partial trace over $W_{\yc}$), we can freely symmetrize the copies of $A$ and $B$.
    Note also that by the binomial theorem,
    \begin{equation}
        \frac{\tilde \pi_{\yc}(A+B)}{c!} \geq (\nu^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb})^{\dagger}\left(\frac{\tilde \pi_{\ya}(A)}{a!} \ot \frac{\tilde \pi_{\yb}(B)}{b!}\right)(\nu^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}).
    \end{equation}
    or equivalently,
    \begin{equation}
        (\nu^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}) \frac{\tilde \pi_{\yc}(A+B)}{c!}(\nu^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb})^{\dagger} \geq P^{\nu;\yc}_{\ya,\yb}\left(\frac{\tilde \pi_{\ya}(A)}{a!} \ot \frac{\tilde \pi_{\yb}(B)}{b!}\right)P^{\nu;\yc}_{\ya,\yb}.
    \end{equation}
    It is tempting to try and convert this operator inequality into an inequality on the operator norms.
    This almost works, but unfortunately, even if the twirled highest weight vectors $v_{\ya}^{U} \in V_{\ya}^n$ and $v_{\yb}^{V} \in V_{\yb^n}$ have unit norm, the projection of their tensor product into $V_{\yc}^n$ does not necessarily have unit norm (unless $U=V$).

    To better understand the meaning of the quantity $W_{\ea,\eb;\ec}^{(c)}$, consider $(x,y,z) \in \mathbb R_{\geq 0}^{3}$ such that $x+y=z$.
    Then
    \begin{align}
        W_{xI/n,yI/n;zI/n}^{(c)} 
        &= \sum_{a+b=c}\frac{c!}{a!b!}\frac{x^ay^b}{z^c} \frac{1}{n^{a+b+c}} \sum_{\substack{\ya\vdash a,\yb \vdash b \\ \yc\vdash c}} \Tr[P_{\ya}]\Tr[P_{\yb}]\Tr[P_{\yc}]\frac{\dim (V_{\yc}^n \ot C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb})}{\dim (V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n})} \\
        &= \sum_{a+b=c}\frac{c!}{a!b!}\frac{x^ay^b}{z^c} \frac{1}{n^{a+b+c}} \sum_{\substack{\ya\vdash a,\yb \vdash b \\ \yc\vdash c}} \dim(W_{\ya}\ot W_{\yb} \ot W_{\yc}) \dim (V_{\yc}^n)^2 c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \\
        &= \sum_{a+b=c}\frac{c!}{a!b!}\frac{x^ay^b}{z^c} \frac{1}{n^{a+b+c}} \sum_{\yc\vdash c} \dim(W_{\yc} \ot V_{\yc}^n)^2 \\
        &= \sum_{\yc\vdash c} \left(\frac{\dim(W_{\yc} \ot V_{\yc}^n)}{n^{c}}\right)^2.
    \end{align}
    Therefore, $W_{xI/n,yI/n;zI/n}^{(c)}$ just quantifies the (square of) the $2$-norm of the Schur-Weyl decomposition at order $c$.
    Alternatively, we have
    \begin{align}
        F_{xI/n,yI/n;zI/n}^{(c)} = 1.
    \end{align}
\hrule
    Expanding out both sides of the useful inequality above and moving normalizations, we see the inequality is equivalent to
    \begin{equation}
        \sum_{\ya,\yb}c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb} \frac{n^{\abs{\ya}}\dim W_{\ya} \chi_{\ya}(\ea)}{\abs{\ya}!\dim V_{\ya}^{n}}\frac{n^{\abs{\yb}}\dim W_{\yb} \chi_{\yb}(\eb)}{\abs{\yb}!\dim V_{\yb}^{n}} \geq \frac{1}{p_n(c)}\frac{n^{\abs{\yc}}\dim W_{\yc} \chi_{\yc}(\ec)}{\abs{\yc}!\dim V_{\yc}^n}
    \end{equation}
    Amazingly, $\lim_{n\to\infty} \frac{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})}{n^{\abs{\yx}}} = \frac{\dim(W_{\yx})}{\abs{\yx}!}$.
    Therefore, as $c^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}$ is known to eventually be independent of $n$, we have by $n^{\abs{\yc}} = n^{\abs{\ya}}n^{\abs{\yb}}$, the identity
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty} \frac{\dim (V_{\yc}^n \ot C^{\yc}_{\ya\yb})}{\dim (V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n})}= \frac{\abs{\ya}!\abs{\yb}!}{\abs{\yc}!} \frac{c^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}\dim W_{\yc}}{\dim (W_{\ya} \ot W_{\yb})}.
    \end{equation}
    This almost simplifies the inequality as $n\to\infty$, but unfortunately $p_n(c) \to \infty$ and thus the lower-bound trivializes.
