    Using the formula for the Schur-polynomial:
    \begin{equation}
        s_{\yx}(x) = \frac{\det[x_i^{\yx_j+n-j}]}{\det[x_i^{n-j}]}
    \end{equation}
    we note that if $x'_i \coloneqq z - x_i$, then
    \begin{equation}
        s_{\yx}(z-x) = \frac{\det[(z - x_i)^{\yx_j+n-j}]}{\det[(z - x_i)^{n-j}]}
    \end{equation}
    Using our binomial theorem, we have for $\yx \vdash k$, where
    \begin{align}
        \tilde \chi_{\yx}(zI-X) 
        &= \sum_{j=0}^{k} \binom{k}{j} \Tr[P_{\yx} ((zI)^{\ot k-j}\ot (-X)^{\ot j})] \\
        &= \sum_{j=0}^{k} \binom{k}{j} (-1)^{j}z^{k-j} \Tr[P_{\yx} (I^{\ot k-j} \ot X^{\ot j})] \\
        &= \sum_{j=0}^{k} \binom{k}{j} (-1)^{j}z^{k-j} \sum_{\mu\vdash j} m^{\mu}_{\yx} \frac{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})}{\dim(V_{\mu}^{n})} \Tr[P_{\mu}X^{\ot j}]
    \end{align}
    where $\Tr_{k-j}[P_{\yx}] = \sum_{\mu\vdash j} L^{\yx}_{\mu} P_{\mu}$ with 
    \begin{equation}
        L^{\yx}_{\mu} = \frac{\Tr[P_{\yx}(I^{\ot k-j}\ot P_{\mu})]}{\Tr[P_{\mu}]} =  m^{\mu}_{\yx}\frac{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})}{\dim(V_{\mu}^{n})}
    \end{equation}
    where $m^{\mu}_{\yx} = \dim(\Hom_{S_j}(W_{\mu}, \Res^{S_k}_{S_j} W_{\yx}))$ (noting that $m^{\mu}_{\yx} = 0$ if $\mu$ is not a subdiagram of $\yx$ denoted by $\mu \subseteq \yx$.
    Therefore,
    \begin{equation}
        s_{\yx}(z-x) = \sum_{j=0}^{k} \binom{k}{j} (-1)^{j}z^{k-j} \sum_{\mu\vdash j} \frac{m^{\mu}_{\yx} \dim(W_{\mu})}{\dim(W_{\yx})} \frac{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})}{\dim(V_{\mu}^{n})} s_{\mu}(x)
    \end{equation}
    \begin{equation}
        s_{\yx}(z-x) = \sum_{\mu \subseteq \yx} \binom{\abs{\yx}}{\abs{\mu}} (-1)^{\abs{\mu}}z^{\abs{\yx}-\abs{\mu}} \frac{m^{\mu}_{\yx} \dim(W_{\mu})}{\dim(W_{\yx})} \frac{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})}{\dim(V_{\mu}^{n})} s_{\mu}(x)
    \end{equation}
    Now let $\yx^{*}$ denote the complementary Young diagram to $\yx$ inside a $n\times \lambda_1$ rectangular Young diagram thus ensuring $V_{\yx^{*}}^n \ot V_{\yx}^n$ contains the trivial representation and thus $(V_{\yx^{*}}^{n})^{*} \simeq V_{\yx}^{n}$ is an isomorphism of $\U(n)$ representations.
    As $\abs{\yx^{*}} = n\lambda_1 - \abs{\lambda}$, we have
    \begin{equation}
        s_{\yx^{*}}(z-x) = \sum_{\mu \subseteq \yx^{*}} \binom{\abs{\yx^{*}}}{\abs{\mu}} (-1)^{\abs{\mu}}z^{\abs{\yx^{*}}-\abs{\mu}} \frac{m^{\mu}_{\yx^{*}} \dim(W_{\mu})}{\dim(W_{\yx^{*}})} \frac{\dim(V_{\yx^{*}}^{n})}{\dim(V_{\mu}^{n})} s_{\mu}(x)
    \end{equation}
    TC: At this stage, it is not clear to me if there is an elegant (non-asymptotic) formula relating $s_{\yx^{*}}(z-x)$ to $s_{\yx}(x)$ for any value of $z$.
\hrule
    In this section, we recalle the fundamental bridge between algebraic objects and geometric objects which serves as Kirillov's orbit method and the more elementary form of geometric quantization.
    In \cite{coquereaux2019revisiting}, \citeauthor{coquereaux2019revisiting} derives a formula relating the character $\chi_\yx(e^{iX})$ for an irreducible representation labelled by $\yx$ evaluated on $e^{iX} \in T$ to the Fourier transform of the orbit measure $\hat \mu_{\ex}(X) \coloneqq \int_{\mathcal O_{\ex}} \exp(i \braket{Y, X}) \diff \mu_{\ex}(Y)$.
    This formula is simple and exact and is the cosequence of a number of well-known formulas.
    First is the Weyl-Character formula in terms of the Weyl-vector $\rho = (n-1, n-2, \ldots, 1, 0)$,
    \begin{equation}
        \chi_{\yx}(e^{ix}) = \frac{\det[e^{ix_i(\yx_j+\rho_j)}]}{\det[e^{ix_i\rho_j}]},
        \qquad
        \chi_{\yx}(x) = \frac{\det[x_i^{\yx_j+\rho_j}]}{\det[x_i^{\rho_j}]},
    \end{equation}
    where $\det[e^{ix_i(\rho_j)}] = \Delta(e^{ix_i}) = \prod_{i<j} (e^{ix_i}-e^{ix_j})$ is the Weyl denominator and $\Delta$ the Vandermonde determinant, defined by the formula:
    \begin{equation}
        \Delta(x) = \det[x_i^{\rho_j}] = \det[x_i^{n-j}] = \prod_{i<j}(x_i-x_j).
    \end{equation}
    Weyl's dimension formula is then 
    \begin{equation}
        \dim(V_{\yx}^n) = \chi_{\yx}(e) = \frac{\Delta(\yx+\rho)}{\Delta(\rho)},
    \end{equation}
    where $\Delta(\rho) = (n-1)!! = (n-1)!(n-2)!\cdots 3!2!1!$ is the super factorial of $n-1$.
    The second formula is the HCIZ integral formula:
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal H(x,y) = \int \diff U \exp(\Tr[xU^{-1}yU]) = \frac{\det[e^{x_iy_j}]\Delta(\rho)}{\Delta(x)\Delta(y)}.
    \end{equation}
    Numerous proofs for this formula can be found in \cite{mcswiggen2021harish}, such as the particularly elgant proof using the Cauchy-Binet formula due to \cite{balantekin2000character}.
    By a direct inspection, one derives an exact correspondence \cite[Equation 18]{coquereaux2018orbital}:
    \begin{equation}
        \Delta(e^{ix})\chi_{\yx}(e^{ix}) = \det[e^{ix_i(\yx_j+\rho_j)}] = \dim(V_{\yx}^{n})\Delta(ix) \mathcal H(ix,\yx+\rho).
    \end{equation}
    It is this formula through which \citeauthor{coquereaux2020horn} derive a direct correspondence between the Littlewood-Richardson coefficients (which is a integral over the compact torus of a product of three characters) and the Horn probability density function for the eigenvalues $\ec$ of the random matrix $C$ distributed such that $C=A+B$ for $(A,B)$ independent with eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb)$ (i.e. the Duistermaat-Heckman measure) (which is an integral over the non-compact Lie algebra of Hermitian matrices of a product of three HCIZ formulas).

    Another interesting identity is following formula:
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal H(x,y) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{1}{k!} \sum_{\mu\vdash k} \frac{\Tr[P_{\mu}x^{\ot k}]\Tr[P_{\mu}y^{\ot k}]}{\Tr[P_{\mu}]},
    \end{equation}
    which can be simplified using the identity
    \begin{equation}
        \frac{1}{\abs{\yx}!}\frac{\dim(W_{\yx})}{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})} = \frac{\Delta(\rho)}{\prod_j(\yx+\rho)_j!},
    \end{equation}
    to obtain
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal H(x,y) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\mu\vdash k} \frac{\Delta(\rho)}{\prod_j(\mu+\rho)_j!} \chi_{\mu}(x)\chi_{\mu}(y).
    \end{equation}
    and also
    \begin{equation}
        \frac{\chi_{\yx}(e^{ix})}{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})} = \frac{\Delta(ix)}{\Delta(e^{ix})} \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\mu\vdash k} \frac{\chi_{\mu}(ix)}{\dim(V_{\mu}^{n})} \frac{\det[(\yx+\rho)_i^{(\mu+\rho)_j}]}{\prod_j(\mu+\rho)_j!}.
    \end{equation}
\hrule
    The Plancherel theorem for compact groups states that for $f \in \mathcal L^{2}(G)$, and $h \in G$, one has an expansion:
    \begin{equation}
        f(h) = \sum_{\yx \in \hat G} \dim V_{\yx} \int \diff g f(g) \chi_{\yx}(gh^{-1}).
    \end{equation}
    This follows from the Peter-Weyl theorem which states that the rescaled matrix coefficients
    \begin{equation}
       \varphi^{\yx}_{ij}(g) \coloneqq \sqrt{\dim V_{\yx}} \pi_{\yx}(g)_{ij},
    \end{equation}
    as $\yx \in \hat G$ varies over all finite dimensional irreducible unitary representations of $G$ and $i,j \in \{1, \ldots, \dim V_{\yx}\}$,
    form an orthonormal basis over $\mathcal L^{2}(G)$ with respect to the Haar-measure induced inner product.
    Let $Z(g,h)$ be an integration kernel defined by
    \begin{align}
        Z(g,h) 
        &= \sum_{\yx \in \hat G} \dim V_{\yx} \chi_{\yx}(gh^{-1})
        = \sum_{\yx \in \hat G} \sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim V_{\yx}} \varphi_{ij}^{\yx}(g) \varphi_{ij}^{\yx}(h)^{*},
    \end{align}
    noting that $Z(g,h)$ can be loosely interpreted as the character of the regular representation of $G$ at $gh^{-1}$.\footnote{TC: I am using $Z$ here because it can also be interpreted as the zero-temperature $t \to \infty$ limit of the heat kernel on $G$ \cite{mcswiggen2021harish,fegan1978heat}.}
    Then the Plancherel identity can be written as
    \begin{equation}
        f(h) = \int \diff g f(g) Z(g,h)
    \end{equation}
    or otherwise $\diff \delta_h(g) = \diff g Z(g,h)$ is the Dirac-delta measure concentrated at the point $h \in G$.
    In the case of $G = \U(n)$, $\hat G$ is the set of all Young diagrams with at most $n$ rows.
    While the integration Kernel $Z(g,h)$ divergences in this case, one can consider the homogeneous degree $k$ component of $Z(g,h)$ defined by a sum over all Young diagrams with $k$ boxes:
    \begin{equation}
        Z_k(g,h) = \sum_{\yx \vdash k} \dim V_{\yx}^{n} \chi_{\yx}(h^{-1}g) 
    \end{equation}
    This makes sense because when $\yx \vdash k$, $V_{\yx}^{n}$ supports an irreducible, unitary, polynomial representation $\pi_{\yx}(g)$ of $\U(n)$ where the matrix coefficients of $\pi_{\yx}(g)$ are homogeneous degree-$k$ polynomials in the matrix coefficients of $g \in U(n)$ and thus $Z_k(g,e)$ is also a homogeneous degree $k$ polynomial in $g$.
    Letting $\tilde K(g,h;t)$ be defined by
    \begin{equation}
        \tilde K(g,h;t) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} Z_{k}(g,h) e^{-tk} = \sum_{\yx}\dim V_{\yx}^{n} \chi_{\yx}(h^{-1}g) e^{-t \abs{\yx}},
    \end{equation}
    we see that $\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\tilde K(g,h;t) = Z(g,h)$ and that $Z_{k}(g,h) = \frac{1}{(-k)^{k}}\partial_{t}^{k} \tilde K(g,h;t) |_{t = 0}$.
     
    Now assuming we can freely extend these functions on $\U(n)$ to functions on arbitrary matrices, we might write (or rather define) for $n\times n$ matrices $X,Y$, the quantity:
    \begin{equation}
        Z_k(X,Y) \coloneqq \sum_{\yx \vdash k} \sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim V_{\yx}^{n}} \dim V_{\yx}^{n} \pi_{\yx}(X)_{ij} \pi_{\yx}(Y)_{ij}^{*}.
    \end{equation}
    One might conjecture that this quantity, or at least its sum over all $k$, also behaves like a Dirac delta measure which detects whether or not $X = Y$.

    Our goal will be to apply this quantity to the Horn problem to detect wether $A+B = C$:
    \begin{equation}
        Z_k(A+B,C) \coloneqq \sum_{\yx \vdash k} \sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim V_{\yx}^{n}} \dim V_{\yx}^{n} \pi_{\yx}(A+B)_{ij} \pi_{\yx}(C)_{ij}^{*}.
    \end{equation}
    In order to derive a test for the eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb,\ec)$ of $(A,B,C)$, we consider integrating over orbits to obtain $W_k(\ea,\eb;\ec)$.
    Using the binomial theorem and Schur-lemma we obtain the formula:
    \begin{align}
        W_k(\ea,\eb;\ec) 
        &\coloneqq \iiint \diff U \diff V \diff W Z_k(U\cdot \ea+V\cdot \eb,W\cdot \ec), \\
        &= \sum_{\yc \vdash k} \dim V_{\yc}^2 \sum_{\ya,\yb} c^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb} \left(\frac{\abs{\yc}!}{\abs{\ya}!\abs{\yb}!}\frac{\dim (W_{\ya} \ot W_{\yb} \ot W_{\yc})}{\dim W_{\yc}^2} \frac{\chi_{\ya}(\ea)\chi_{\yb}(\eb)\chi_{\yc}(\ec)}{\dim (V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n} \ot V_{\yc}^{n})}\right).
    \end{align}
    TC: Note this formula is almost the same as the quantity defined above, except for normalizing the eigenvalues and for the additional factors of $1/\dim(W_{\yc})^{2}$.

    One could correct for this by considering a quantity
    \begin{equation}
        Z_k'(g,h) \coloneqq \sum_{\yx \vdash k} \sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim V_{\yx}^{n}} \dim V_{\yx}^{n}(\dim W_{\yx})^2 \pi_{\yx}(g)_{ij} \pi_{\yx}(h)_{ij}^{*}.
    \end{equation}
    which has $Z_k'(g,e)$ behaving like the character of the representation $\bigoplus_{\yx \vdash k} V_{\yx}^{\oplus m_{\yx}}$ with multiplicity $m_{\yx} = \dim V_{\yx}^{n}(\dim W_{\yx})^2$.
    Just as $Z_k(g,e)$ is the character of the representation $\bigoplus_{\yx \vdash k} V_{\yx}^{\oplus \dim V_{\yx}}$ which is isomorphic to left-representation of $g$ acting on symmetric representation of $k$ copies of a bipartite system. 
    TC: There is more to work out here!
\hrule
    TC: There are a few ways to recover the Littlewood-Richarson coefficients from the $6j$-symbols.
    For example, after setting $\yd$ to the empty Young diagram (which we denote by $\emptyyoung$), corresponding to the representation space $V_{\emptyyoung}^{n}$ which supports the trivial irreducible representation of $\U(n)$ of dimension one, the $6j$-symbols simplify:
    \begin{equation}
        R^{\ye,n}_{\ya,\yb,\yd}\norm{\abcdefsymbol}_2^2 
        \stackrel{\yd=\emptyyoung}{\longrightarrow} 
        \delta_{\ye,\yc}\delta_{\yf,\yb} R^{\yc,n}_{\ya,\yb,\emptyyoung}\norm{\abcdefsymboldempty}_2^2
        =\delta_{\ye,\yc}\delta_{\yf,\yb} \frac{\dim V_{\yc}^{n} c^{\yc}_{\ya,\yb}}{\dim (V_{\ya}^{n} \ot V_{\yb}^{n})}
    \end{equation}
\hrule

\subsection{Necessary and sufficient conditions}

Throughout this section, we will consider a candidate sextuple of eigenvalues, denoted by $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ and will assume they are associated to non-zero positive semidefinite $n\times n$ matrices, typically denoted by $(A,B,C,D,E,F)$.
While we do not necessarily assume the candidate eigenvalues to be tetrahedral, we will assume they at least satisfy the trace conditions:
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:tet_eig_trace_cond}
    \etr{\ec} = \etr{\ea} + \etr{\eb}, \quad
    \etr{\ef} = \etr{\eb} + \etr{\ed}, \quad
    \etr{\ee} = \etr{\ec} + \etr{\ed}, \quad \text{and} \quad
    \etr{\ee} = \etr{\ea} + \etr{\ef}.
\end{equation}

\begin{align}
    \disttetalt
    &\coloneqq \min_{\substack{ABC\\DEF}} \max\left\{\norm{\eig(\nrm{A+B})-\eig(\nrm C)}_1, \norm{\eig(\nrm{B+D})-\eig(\nrm{F})}_1, \norm{\eig(\nrm{A+B+D})-\eig(\nrm{E})}_1\right\}
\end{align}
where the minimum is taken over all $(A,B,C,D,E,F)$ with eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$.
Note that $\disttetalt \geq 0$ holds with equality if and only if the eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ are tetrahedral.

The purpose of this section is to produce a family of \textit{necessary} conditions for $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ to be tetrahedral, and furthermore that the conjunction of these necessary conditions is actually \textit{sufficient}.
These conditions take the form of a lower bound on a quantity (to be defined shortly) which is interpreted as the expected value of the squared norm $\norm{\abcdefsymbol}_2^2$ of the $6j$ symbol when the Young diagrams $(\ya, \yb, \yc, \yd, \ye, \yf)$ are sampled randomly from the sets of Young diagrams with fixed sizes $(a,b,c,d,e,f)$ subject to the contraints 
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:yd_size_cond}
    c=a+b, \quad f=b+d, \quad e=c+d, \quad \text{and} \quad e=a+f.
\end{equation}
More specifically, these conditions are indexed by a positive integer $k$ which determines the sizes $(a,b,c,d,e,f)$ of the Young diagrams $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ in a manner which ensures the relative proportions of the Young diagram sizes, $(a,b,c,d,e,f)$, are approximately $k$ times the relative proportions of the eigenvalue traces $(\etr \ea, \etr \eb, \etr \ec, \etr \ed, \etr \ee, \etr \ef)$.
Specifically, $a,b$, and $d$ are taken to be
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:yd_ceil_cond}
    a = \ceil{k\frac{\etr{\ea}}{\etr{\ee}}}, \qquad
    b = \ceil{k\frac{\etr{\eb}}{\etr{\ee}}}, \qquad
    d = \ceil{k\frac{\etr{\ed}}{\etr{\ee}}},
\end{equation}
while $c,e$ and $f$ are then fixed by \cref{eq:yd_size_cond}.

Furthermore, we define three polynomials in $k$:
\begin{equation}
    t(k) = \frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{11^3}(k+2), \qquad y_r(k) = (k+3)^{r-1}, \qquad m_n(k) = (k+3)^{n(n-1)/2}.
\end{equation}
Our main theorem then concerns the following quantity,
\begin{equation}
    \wksixj \coloneqq \sum_{\substack{\ya\yb\yc\\\yd\ye\yf}} \tilde \chi_\ya(\nrm A)\tilde \chi_\yb(\nrm B)\tilde \chi_\yc(\nrm C)\tilde \chi_\yd(\nrm D)\tilde \chi_\ye(\nrm E)\tilde \chi_\yf(\nrm F) \norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{2}^2,
\end{equation}
which is equal to the expected value of the squared Hilbert-Schmidt norm of the $6j$ symbol when the Young diagrams $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ are sampled independently from each other according to their corresponding Schur-Weyl distributions.
Our first result provides a lower bound on the value of $\wk$.
\begin{thm}
    \label{thm:wk_lower}
    If $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ are tetrahedral eigenvalues with maximum rank $r = \rank(\ee)$, then
    \begin{equation}
        \wksixj \geq \frac{1}{t(k)^3}\frac{1}{y_r(k)^{24}}.
    \end{equation}
\end{thm}
Our second result provides a complementary upper bound on the value of $\wk$.
\begin{thm}
    \label{thm:wk_upper}
    Given candidate eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \wksixj \leq t(k)y_{r}(k)^6m_n(k)^6\exp\left(-k\frac{\tau_{\min}}{\tau_{\max}} \frac{1}{10}\disttetalt^2\right)
    \end{equation}
\end{thm}
When \cref{thm:wk_lower,thm:wk_upper} are taken together, one concludes that if $\disttetalt = \Delta > 0$, then for sufficiently large $k$ such that $\frac{k}{\log k} \gtrsim \frac{1}{\Delta^2}$, the inequality in \cref{thm:wk_lower} becomes violated.
\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:wk_lower}]
    Given a positive semidefinite $n\times n$ matrices $A,B,D$, define
    \begin{align}
        \TetGammaOpt{A,B,D}{\ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd & \ye & \yf} 
        &\coloneqq \Tr[(A^{\ot a} \ot B^{\ot b} \ot D^{\ot d}) \Pi_{\ya}\Pi_{\yb}\Pi_{\yc}\Pi_{\yd}\Pi_{\ye}\Pi_{\yf}].
    \end{align}
    \begin{align}
        Q\sixjargs 
        &\coloneqq \Tr\left[\left(\frac{A^{\ot \abs{\ya}}}{\abs{\ya}!} \ot \frac{B^{\ot \abs{\yb}}}{\abs{\yb}!} \ot \frac{D^{\ot \abs{\yd}}}{\abs{\yd}!}\right) (P_{\ya}\ot P_{\yb} \ot P_{\yd})(P_{\yc}\ot \id^{\ot \abs{\yd}})P_{\ye}(\id^{\ot \abs{\ya}} \ot P_{\yf})\right], \\
        &= \Tr\left[\left(\Phi_{\ya}(A) \ot \Phi_{\yb}(B) \ot \Phi_{\yd}(D)\right) (P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}\ot \id_{V_{\yd}^{n}})P^{\ye}_{\ya\yb\yd}(\id_{V_{\ya}^{n}} \ot P^{\yf}_{\yb\yd})\right].
    \end{align}
    \begin{equation}
        \sum_{\yx\vdash k}\varphi_{\yx}(X) = \frac{\Tr(X)^{k}}{k!},
        \qquad
        \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\yx\vdash k}\varphi_{\yx}(X) = \exp(\Tr(X)),
    \end{equation}
    \begin{align}
        \sum_{\ye\vdash \abs{\ya}+\abs{\yb}+\abs{\yd}}\sum_{\yc\vdash \abs{\ya}+\abs{\yb}}\sum_{\yf\vdash \abs{\yb}+\abs{\yd}}Q\sixjargs &= \varphi_{\ya}(A)\varphi_{\yb}(B)\varphi_{\yd}(D), \\
        \sum_{\ye\vdash \abs{\ya}+\abs{\yb}+\abs{\yd}}\sum_{\yf\vdash \abs{\yb}+\abs{\yd}}Q\sixjargs &= \Tr[(\Phi_{\ya}(A) \ot \Phi_{\yb}(B)) P^{\yc}_{\ya\yb}]\varphi_{\yd}(D), \\
        \sum_{a+b=\abs{\yc}}\sum_{\ya\vdash a}\sum_{\yb\vdash b}\sum_{\ye\vdash a+b+\abs{\yd}}\sum_{\yf\vdash b+\abs{\yd}}Q\sixjargs &= \varphi_{\yc}(A+B)\varphi_{\yd}(D), \\
        \sum_{b+d=\abs{\yf}}\sum_{\yb\vdash b}\sum_{\yd\vdash d}\sum_{\ye\vdash \abs{\ya}+b+d}\sum_{\yc\vdash \abs{\ya}+b}Q\sixjargs &= \varphi_{\ya}(A)\varphi_{\yf}(B+D), \\
        \sum_{\ya\vdash a}\sum_{\yb\vdash b}\sum_{\yd \vdash d}\sum_{\ye\vdash a+b+d}\sum_{\yc\vdash a+b}\sum_{\yf\vdash b+d}Q\sixjargs &= \frac{\Tr(A)^{a}}{a!}\frac{\Tr(B)^{b}}{b!}\frac{\Tr(D)^{d}}{d!}, \\
        \sum_{a+b+d=k}\sum_{\ya\vdash a}\sum_{\yb\vdash b}\sum_{\yd \vdash d}\sum_{\ye\vdash a+b+d}\sum_{\yc\vdash a+b}\sum_{\yf\vdash b+d}Q\sixjargs &= \frac{\Tr(A+B+D)^{k}}{k!}, \\
        \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{a+b+d=k}\sum_{\ya\vdash a}\sum_{\yb\vdash b}\sum_{\yd \vdash d}\sum_{\ye\vdash a+b+d}\sum_{\yc\vdash a+b}\sum_{\yf\vdash b+d}Q\sixjargs &= \exp(\Tr(A+B+D)), \\
        \sum_{\yc\yf\ya\yb\yd}Q\sixjargs &= \varphi_{\ye}(A+B+D).
    \end{align}
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb E_{A,B,D}Q\sixjargs = \varphi_{\ya}(A)\varphi_{\yb}(B)\varphi_{\yd}(D) R^{\ye,n}_{\ya,\yb,\yd}\norm{\abcdefsymbol}_2^2,
    \end{equation}
    The following bounds hold:
    \begin{align}
        \abs{Q\sixjargs} &\leq
        \varphi_{\ya}(A)\varphi_{\yb}(B)\varphi_{\yd}(D) \norm{\abcdefsymbol}_2, \\
        \abs{Q\sixjargs} &\leq
        \varphi_{\ye}(A+B+D) \\
        \abs{Q\sixjargs} &\leq
        \varphi_{\ya}(A)\varphi_{\yb}(B)\varphi_{\yd}(D), \\
        \abs{Q\sixjargs} &\leq
        \left(\varphi_{\ya}(A)\varphi_{\yf}(B+D)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
        \left(\varphi_{\yc}(A+B)\varphi_{\yd}(D)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
    \end{align}
    After normalizing the positive semidefinite matrices $A,B,D$ to $\nrm A, \nrm B, \nrm D$, relaxing some instances of $\tilde \chi_\yx(\nrm X) \leq 1$, and using the $k$-dependent sizes of Young diagrams, $(a,b,c,d,e,f)$, one obtains the bound
    %\begin{equation}
    %    \frac{1}{t(k)^3}\abs{\TetGammaOpt{\nrm A,\nrm B,\nrm D}{\ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd & \ye & \yf}}^3 \leq
    %    \tilde \chi_{\yc}(\nrm{A+B})\tilde \chi_{\yf}(\nrm{B+D})\tilde \chi_{\ye}(\nrm{A+B+D})
    %\end{equation}
    %and also
    %\begin{equation}
    %    \frac{1}{t(k)^3}\abs{\TetGammaOpt{\nrm A,\nrm B,\nrm D}{\ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd & \ye & \yf}}^4 \leq
    %    \tilde \chi_{\ya}(\nrm A)\tilde \chi_{\yb}(\nrm B)\tilde \chi_{\yc}(\nrm{A+B})\tilde \chi_{\yd}(\nrm D)\tilde \chi_{\yf}(\nrm{B+D})\tilde \chi_{\ye}(\nrm{A+B+D})
    %\end{equation}
    %and also
    \begin{equation}
        \frac{1}{t(k)^3}\abs{\TetGammaOpt{\nrm A,\nrm B,\nrm D}{\ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd & \ye & \yf}}^5 \leq
        \tilde \chi_{\ya}(\nrm A)\tilde \chi_{\yb}(\nrm B)\tilde \chi_{\yc}(\nrm{A+B})\tilde \chi_{\yd}(\nrm D)\tilde \chi_{\yf}(\nrm{B+D})\tilde \chi_{\ye}(\nrm{A+B+D})\norm{\abcdefsymbol}_2^2
    \end{equation}
    where the factor $t(k)$ is the polynomial expression emerging from the trinomial distribution lower bound from \cref{lem:multinomial_dist_lower_bound} due to \cref{eq:yd_size_cond} and \cref{eq:yd_ceil_cond} and
    \begin{align}
        \frac{1}{t(k)} \leq \frac{e!}{a!b!d!} \frac{\etr{\ea}^{a}\etr{\eb}^{b}\etr{\ed}^{d}}{\etr{\ee}^{e}} &\leq \min\left\{\frac{c!}{a!b!} \frac{\etr{\ea}^{a}\etr{\eb}^{b}}{\etr{\ec}^{c}}, \frac{f!}{b!d!} \frac{\etr{\eb}^{b}\etr{\ed}^{d}}{\etr{\ef}^{f}} \right\}.
    \end{align}
    By assumption, $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ are tetrahedral meaning there exists $A,B,D$ such that the above inequality implies
    \begin{equation}
        \wksixj \geq \frac{1}{t(k)^3}\sum_{\substack{\ya\yb\yc\\\yd\ye\yf}}\abs{\TetGammaOpt{\nrm A,\nrm B,\nrm D}{\ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd & \ye & \yf}}^5.
    \end{equation}
    Now as defined, $\TetGammaOpt{\nrm A,\nrm B,\nrm D}{\ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd & \ye & \yf}$ need not be a real number;
    nevertheless it is still normalized in the sense that
    \begin{equation}
        \sum_{\substack{\ya\yb\yc\\\yd\ye\yf}}\TetGammaOpt{\nrm A,\nrm B,\nrm D}{\ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd & \ye & \yf} = 1.
    \end{equation}
    Given any collection of $s$ non-zero complex numbers $(z_1, \ldots, z_s)$ summing to one, $\sum_{i=1}^{s}z_i = 1$, one can show $\sum_{i=1}^{s}\abs{z_i}^p$ has minimum value $s \frac{1}{s^p} = \frac{1}{s^{p-1}}$ and is achieved when $z_i=\frac{1}{s}$.
    In this case, the number of possible Young diagrams for which $\TetGammaOpt{\nrm A,\nrm B,\nrm D}{\ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd & \ye & \yf}$ is non-zero is upper bounded by $y_{r}(k)^6$ and therefore the claim holds.
\end{proof}
\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:wk_upper}]
    Given the integral formula,
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb E_{A,B,D}\TetGammaOpt{A,B,D}{\ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd & \ye & \yf} = \tilde \chi_{\ya}(A)\tilde \chi_{\yb}(B)\tilde \chi_{\yd}(D) R^{\ye,n}_{\ya,\yb,\yd}\norm{\abcdefsymbol}_2^2,
    \end{equation}
    we obtain,
    \begin{align}
        &\tilde \chi_{\ya}(\nrm A)\tilde \chi_{\yb}(\nrm B)\tilde \chi_{\yc}(\nrm{C})\tilde \chi_{\yd}(\nrm D)\tilde \chi_{\yf}(\nrm F)\tilde \chi_{\ye}(\nrm E)\norm{\abcdefsymbol}_2^2 \\
        &\quad \leq R^{\ye,n;-1}_{\ya,\yb,\yd} \tilde \chi_{\yc}(\nrm{C})\tilde \chi_{\yf}(\nrm{F})\tilde \chi_{\ye}(\nrm{E}) \mathbb E_{A,B,D} \abs{\TetGammaOpt{\nrm A,\nrm B,\nrm D}{\ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd & \ye & \yf}} \\
        &\quad \leq t(k)R^{\ye,n;-1}_{\ya,\yb,\yd}\tilde \chi_{\yc}(\nrm{C})\tilde \chi_{\yf}(\nrm{F})\tilde \chi_{\ye}(\nrm{E}) \mathbb E_{ABD} \tilde \chi_{\yc}(\nrm{A+B})^{\frac{1}{3}}\tilde \chi_{\yf}(\nrm{B+D})^{\frac{1}{3}}\tilde \chi_{\ye}(\nrm{A+B+D})^{\frac{1}{3}}.
    \end{align}
    Now using the eigenvalue separation lemma, which states,
    \begin{equation}
        \tilde \chi_{\yx}(\nrm X)^p\tilde \chi_{\yx}(\nrm X)^q \leq \dim(V_{\yx}^n)^{p+q} \exp(-\abs{\yx}\frac{pq}{2(p+q)} \norm{\eig(\nrm X)-\eig(\nrm Y)}^{2}_1),
    \end{equation}
    we conclude (using $p=1$, and $q=\frac{1}{4}$, and that $\abs{\yx} \geq k \frac{\tau_{\min}}{\tau_{\max}}$),
    \begin{align}
        &\tilde \chi_{\ya}(\nrm A)\tilde \chi_{\yb}(\nrm B)\tilde \chi_{\yc}(\nrm{C})\tilde \chi_{\yd}(\nrm D)\tilde \chi_{\yf}(\nrm F)\tilde \chi_{\ye}(\nrm E)\norm{\abcdefsymbol}_2^2 \\
        &\quad\leq t(k)R^{\ye,n;-1}_{\ya,\yb,\yd}\dim(V_{\yc}^{n}\ot V_{\ye}^{n} \ot V_{\yf}^{n})^{\frac{5}{4}} \exp\left(-k\frac{\tau_{\min}}{\tau_{\max}} \frac{1}{10}\disttetalt^2\right).
    \end{align}
    Finally, the claim holds because $1 \leq \dim(V_{\yx}^{n}) \leq m_n(k)$ for all $\yx \in \{\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf\}$ and the exponents sum to $1+1+1+\frac{5}{4}+\frac{1}{4}+\frac{5}{4} = \frac{23}{4} < 6$ and there are at most $y_{r}(k)^6$ summands.
\end{proof}

\clearpage
{\color{green!50!black}
    TC: THIS IS A POTENTIAL ALTERNATIVE USING A DIFFERENCE DISTANCE \\
    To quantify the distance from $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ to the closed set of tetrahedral eigenvalues, we use the following distance measure:
    \begin{align}
        \disttet
        &\coloneqq \min_{\substack{\tilde \ea \tilde \eb \tilde \ec\tilde\ed \tilde \ee \tilde \ef\\\mathrm{tetrahedral}\\\forall \ex : \abs{\tilde \ex}=\abs{\ex}}} 
        \max\{
            \norm{\nea - \tilde{\nea}}_{1},
            \norm{\neb - \tilde{\neb}}_{1},
            \norm{\nec - \tilde{\nec}}_{1},
            \norm{\ned - \tilde{\ned}}_{1},
            \norm{\nee - \tilde{\nee}}_{1},
            \norm{\nef - \tilde{\nef}}_{1}
        \},
    \end{align}
    where the minimum is taken over all sextuples $(\tilde{\ea},\tilde{\eb},\tilde{\ec},\tilde{\ed},\tilde{\ee},\tilde{\ef})$ of tetrahedral eigenvalues subject to the constraint that the respective traces are equal:
    \begin{equation}
        \etr{\tilde{\ea}} = \etr{\ea}, \quad
        \etr{\tilde{\eb}} = \etr{\eb}, \quad
        \etr{\tilde{\ec}} = \etr{\ec}, \quad
        \etr{\tilde{\ed}} = \etr{\ed}, \quad
        \etr{\tilde{\ee}} = \etr{\ee}, \quad
        \etr{\tilde{\ef}} = \etr{\ef}.
    \end{equation}
    Evidently, $\disttet \geq 0$ with equality holding if and only if the eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ are tetrahedral.
    Moreover we have $\disttetalt \geq \disttet$.\\

    We will define two probability distributions, $\qk$ and $\pk$.
    These probability distributions are defined over a common set of Young diagrams $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ with sizes $(a,b,c,d,e,f)$ depending on $k$ according to \cref{eq:yd_size_cond} and \cref{eq:yd_ceil_cond}.
    First, $\qk$ is defined by
    \begin{equation}
        \qksixj \coloneqq h^n_\ya h^n_\yb h^n_\yd R^{\ye,n}_{\ya,\yb,\yd} \norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{2}^2.
    \end{equation}
    where
    \begin{equation}
        h^{n}_\yx = \frac{\dim(V_\yx^n)^2}{\dim(V_{(\abs{\yx})}^{n^2})}, \qquad \text{and} \qquad R^{\ye,n}_{\ya,\yb,\yd}\coloneqq \frac{\dim (V_{\ye}^{n})}{\dim (V_{\ya}^n\ot V_{\yb}^{n}\ot V_{\yd}^{n})}.
    \end{equation}
    That $\qk$ is a probability distribution follows from the observation that 
    \begin{equation}
        \sum_{\yc \vdash c}\sum_{\yf \vdash f}\norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{2}^2 = \dim(C^{\ye}_{\ya,\yb,\yd})
    \end{equation}
    where $C^{\ye}_{\ya,\yb,\yd}$ is multiplicity for $V_{\ye}^{n}$ appearing the tensor product $V_{\ya}^n\ot V_{\yb}^{n}\ot V_{\yd}^{n}$.
    Note that $\qk$ depends only on the ratios between the \textit{traces} of the candidate eigenvalues (as they fix the sizes of the involved Young diagrams), but not on the particular eigenvalues themselves.

    Now for each such positive integer $k$, we define a distribution $\pk$, as the product distribution:
    \begin{align}
        \pksixj
        &\coloneqq \tilde \chi_\ya(\nrm A)\tilde \chi_\yb(\nrm B)\tilde \chi_\yc(\nrm C)\tilde \chi_\yd(\nrm D)\tilde \chi_\ye(\nrm E)\tilde \chi_\yf(\nrm F),
        %&=\Tr[P_{\ya} \nrm A^{\otimes a}]\Tr[P_{\yb} \nrm B^{\otimes b}]\Tr[P_{\yc} \nrm C^{\otimes c}]\Tr[P_{\yd} \nrm D^{\otimes d}]\Tr[P_{\ye} \nrm E^{\otimes e}]\Tr[P_{\yf} \nrm F^{\otimes f}],
    \end{align}
    where $(A,B,C,D,E,F)$ are any Hermitian operators with eigenvalues $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$.

    We will prove the following theorem regarding the relationship between $\qk$ and $\pk$:
    \begin{thm}
        If $(\ea, \eb, \ec, \ed, \ee, \ef)$ are tetrahedral eigenvalues, then
        \begin{equation}
            \qksixj^4 \leq \sup_{\mathrm{tetra tilde}} t(k)^3 \pksixjt
        \end{equation}
    \end{thm}
\hrule
    One can now combine these separate bounds on $P$ and $Q$ to obtain a bound between $P$ and $Q$.
    Let $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ be the given candidate sextuple of eigenvalues satisfying the trace conditions.
    Then for any $X,Y,Z \geq 0$ positive semidefinite $n\times n$ matrices that are trace one, let
    \begin{equation}
        \begin{alignedat}{3}
            &\tilde A \coloneqq \abs{\ea}X, \qquad
            &&\tilde B \coloneqq \abs{\eb}Y, \qquad
            &&\tilde C \coloneqq \abs{\ea}X+\abs{\eb}Y, \qquad \\
            &\tilde D \coloneqq \abs{\ed}Z, \qquad
            &&\tilde E \coloneqq \abs{\ea}X+\abs{\eb}Y+\abs{\ed}Z, \qquad
            &&\tilde F \coloneqq \abs{\eb}Y + \abs{\ed}Z.
        \end{alignedat}
    \end{equation}
    This ensures that the eigenvalues $(\eat,\ebt,\ect,\edt,\eet,\eft)$ of $(\tilde A, \tilde B, \tilde C, \tilde D, \tilde E, \tilde F)$ are tetrahedral and moreover that the traces of the tetrahedral eigenvalues match the traces of the candidate eigenvalues:
    \begin{equation}
        (\abs \eat,\abs \ebt,\abs \ect,\abs \edt,\abs \eet,\abs \eft) = (\abs \ea, \abs \eb, \abs \ec, \abs \ed, \abs \ee, \abs \ef).
    \end{equation}
    Using the integral formula for $Q$ above, we obtain
    \begin{equation}
        Q\left[\ygrid\right] \leq \sup_{\tilde A,\tilde B,\tilde D} \abs{\TetGammaOpt{\nrm{\tilde A},\nrm{\tilde B},\nrm{\tilde D}}{\ya & \yb & \yc \\ \yd & \ye & \yf}} \leq 12^\frac{7}{4}(k \abs{\ee}+3)^{\frac{2}{4}} \sup_{\tilde A,\tilde B,\tilde D}P\left[\ygrid\middle|\etgrid \right]^{\frac{1}{4}}.
    \end{equation}
    But eigenvalue estimation yields the following bound:
    \begin{equation}
        P\left[\ygrid\middle|\etgrid \right]^{\frac{1}{8}} P\left[\ygrid\middle|\egrid \right]^{\frac{1}{2}} \leq (k\abs{\ee}+4)^{\frac{5}{16}n(n-1)}\exp(-\frac{k}{5} D_{*}\left[\egrid \right] )
    \end{equation}
    This is because Pinsker's inequality yields
    \begin{align}
        p\kl{\nrm \yx}{\nrm \ex} + q\kl{\nrm \yx}{\nrm \ext}
        \geq \frac{p}{2} \norm{\nrm \yx - \nrm \ex}_1^{2} + \frac{q}{2}\norm{\nrm \yx - \nrm \ext}_1^{2},
        \geq \frac{pq}{2(p+q)} \norm{\nrm \ex - \nrm \ext}_1^{2},
    \end{align}
    where the last inequality arises because the weighted sum of squared norms is minimized when $(p+q)\nrm \yx = p \nrm \ex + q \nrm \ext$.
    Now define $D_{*}\left(\egrid \right)$ a non-negative constant which measures the distance from $(\ea, \eb, \ec, \ed, \ee, \ef)$ to the closed set of tetrahedral eigenvalues $(\eat, \ebt, \ect, \edt, \eet, \eft)$ with matching traces (i.e., $\abs{\ea}=\abs{\eat}$, $\abs{\eb}=\abs{\ebt}$, etc.)
    \begin{align}
        \begin{split}
            D_{*}\left(\egrid \right) \coloneqq
            \min_{\substack{\eat \ebt \ect \\ \edt \eet \eft}}
            \bigg(
                &\abs{\ea}\norm{\nea - \tilde{\nea}}_{1}^{2}
                + \abs{\eb}\norm{\neb - \tilde{\neb}}_{1}^{2}
                + \abs{\ec}\norm{\nec - \tilde{\nec}}_{1}^{2} + \cdots \\
                &\cdots + \abs{\ed}\norm{\ned - \tilde{\ned}}_{1}^{2}
                + \abs{\ef}\norm{\nee - \tilde{\nee}}_{1}^{2}
                + \abs{\ee}\norm{\nef - \tilde{\nef}}_{1}^{2}
            \bigg).
        \end{split}
    \end{align}
}

\section{The large \texorpdfstring{$\boldsymbol{n}$}{n} limit \& an entropic inequality}
\label{sec:large_n}

The purpose of this section is to show how to use the necessary condition for $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ to be tetrahedral provided by \cref{cor:tet_sixj_lower_bound} to derive inequalities satisfied by tetrahedral eigenvalues.
Our strategy will be to prove an upper bound the generalized $6j$ symbol which depends only on the dimensions of the underlying irreducible representations and the multiplicity spaces between them.
By combining this upper bound with the lower bound provided by \cref{cor:tet_sixj_lower_bound}, we obtain a new constraint on the convergent sequence of Young diagrams $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$.

\begin{thm}
    \label{thm:sixj_ssa_bound}
    Let $(\ya,\yb,\yc,\yd,\ye,\yf)$ be a sextuple of Young diagrams.
    Then
    \begin{equation}
        \norm{\abcdefsymbol}_{2}^{2}
        \leq \left(c_{\ya,\yf}^{\ye}c_{\yb,\yd}^{\yf}c_{\ya,\yb}^{\yc}c_{\yc,\yd}^{\ye}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \frac{\dim(V_{\yc}^n) \dim(V_{\yf}^n)}{\dim(V_{\yb}^n) \dim(V_{\ye}^n)}.
    \end{equation}
\end{thm}
\begin{proof}
    Here we provide a diagrammatic proof of the claimed inequality.
    For the sake of clarity, we abbreviate the irreducible representation labels, $V_{\yx}^{n}$, and omit the multiplicity space labels on the intermediate steps.
    \begin{equation}
        \tikzfig{tikz/final/sixj_upper}
    \end{equation}
    The first equality follows from \cref{defn:sixj}.
    The inequality follows from an application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality with respect to the annotated contraction.
    The final equality follows from applying Schur's lemma to components of the diagram mapping $V_{\yb}^{n}$ to itself on the left and $V_{\ye}^{n}$ to itself on the right.
    Note that the factors of $\dim(V_{\ye}^{n})^2$ appearing on left-most and right-most sides of the above inequality cancel producing the claimed inequality.
\end{proof}

An immediate application of \cref{thm:sixj_ssa_bound} is to combine it with \cref{cor:tet_sixj_lower_bound}.
If $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ are tetrahedral eigenvalues, then by \cref{cor:tet_sixj_lower_bound} there exists a converging sequence of Young diagrams $(\ya_k, \yb_k, \yc_k, \yd_k, \ye_k, \yf_k)$ such that\footnote{Here we used the well-known inequality $\norm{X}_{\infty} \leq \norm{X}_{2}$.}
\begin{equation}
    \label{eq:combined_bound}
    \frac{1}{q(k)^2} \leq \norm{\tightcurlysixj{ \ya_k & \yb_k & \yc_k \\ \yd_k & \ye_k & \yf_k}}_{2}^2 \leq \left(c_{\ya_k,\yf_k}^{\ye_k}c_{\yb_k,\yd_k}^{\yf_k}c_{\ya_k,\yb_k}^{\yc_k}c_{\yc_k,\yd_k}^{\ye_k}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \frac{\dim(V_{\yc_k}^n) \dim(V_{\yf_k}^n)}{\dim(V_{\yb_k}^n) \dim(V_{\ye_k}^n)}.
\end{equation}
Remarkably, both $q(k)$ (given by \cref{eq:q_of_k}) and the Littlewood-Richardson coefficients $c_{\ya_k,\yf_k}^{\ye_k}, c_{\yb_k,\yd_k}^{\yf_k}, c_{\ya_k,\yb_k}^{\yc_k}$, and $c_{\yc_k,\yd_k}^{\ye_k}$ are \textit{independent} of the dimension parameter $n$ defining the group $\U(n)$ (assuming $n$ is at least as large as the maximum depth of any of the involved Young diagrams, i.e., $n \geq r_{\max}$ where $r_{\max}$ is the maximum rank of any of the tetrahedral eigenvalues).
This observation provides one with an opportunity to optimize the above bound by varying the value of $n$.

In particular, it will useful to better understand how the dimension, $\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})$, of the irreducible representation $V_{\yx}^{n}$ of $\U(n)$ grows as $n$ becomes much larger than the number of rows of $\yx$.
In general, for fixed $\yx$, the function $n \mapsto V_{\yx}^{n}$ can grow at an exponential rate with increasing $n$.
One can find estimates for $\dim(V_{\ex}^{n})$ in the regime where $n$ is large compared to $\abs{\yx}$ and also the last row $\yx_{\min}$ is large compared to the depth of $\yx$ in paper by \Citeauthor{itzykson1966unitary} \cite[Section III]{itzykson1966unitary}.
Here we use an elegant presentation of this scaling, which relates the asymptotics of $\dim(V_{\ex}^{n})$ to the value of $\dim(W_{\ex})$ (see \cite[Lemma 2]{harrow2023approximate} or \cite[Equations (25,26)]{childs2007weak}.
\begin{prop}
    \label{prop:harrow_formula}
    Let $\yx$ be a Young diagram with depth at most $n$. Then
    \begin{equation}
        \frac{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})}{n^{\abs{\yx}}} = \frac{\dim(W_{\yx})}{\abs{\yx}!}\prod_{(i,j) \in \yx} \left(1 + \frac{j-i}{n}\right).
    \end{equation}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    The statement follows from the dimension formulas for $\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})$ and $\dim(W_{\yx})$ in \cref{eq:weyl_dim} and \cref{eq:frob_dim}. See also \cite[Appendix A]{harrow2023approximate}.
    %\begin{align}
    %    \dim(V_{\ex}^{n}) = \prod_{1\leq i < j \leq n}\frac{\tilde \lambda_i - \tilde \lambda_j}{j-i},
    %    \qquad \text{and} \qquad
    %    \dim(W_{\ex}) = \frac{\abs{\ex}!}{\tilde \lambda_1!\cdots \tilde \lambda_n!}\prod_{1\leq i < j \leq n}(\tilde \lambda_i - \tilde \lambda_j),
    %\end{align}
    %where $\tilde \lambda_j \coloneqq \lambda_j+n-j$.
\end{proof}
From \cref{prop:harrow_formula} we obtain the following corollary.
\begin{cor}
    \label{cor:harrow_ssa_lim}
    Let $\yc,\yf,\yb,\ye$ be a quadruple of Young diagrams such that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:balanced_sizes}
        \abs{\yc}+\abs{\yf} = \abs{\yb}+\abs{\ye}.
    \end{equation}
    Then
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}
        \frac{\dim(V_{\yc}^n) \dim(V_{\yf}^n)}{\dim(V_{\yb}^n) \dim(V_{\ye}^n)} =
        \frac{\abs{\yb}!\abs{\ye}!}{\abs{\yc}!\abs{\yf}!}
        \frac{\dim(W_{\yc}) \dim(W_{\yf})}{\dim(W_{\yb}) \dim(W_{\ye})}
    \end{equation}
\end{cor}
\begin{proof}
    From \cref{prop:harrow_formula} one evidently has for any Young diagram $\yx$ the limit
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty} \frac{\dim(V_{\yx}^{n})}{n^{\abs{\yx}}} = \frac{\dim(W_{\yx})}{\abs{\yx}!}.
    \end{equation}
    The claim then follows because the missing prefactor, $\frac{n^{\abs{\yb}}n^{\abs{\ye}}}{n^{\abs{\yc}}n^{\abs{\yf}}}$, is equal to one by \cref{eq:balanced_sizes}.
\end{proof}
We are now in a position to prove the main result of this section.
\begin{thm}
    Let $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ be tetrahedral eigenvalues.
    Then
    \begin{equation}
        I(\ec, \ef; \eb, \ee)  = s(\ec) + s(\ef) - s(\eb) - s(\ee) \geq 0,
    \end{equation}
    where
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:little_s_defn}
        s(\ex) \coloneqq - \sum_{i=1}^{n} \ex_i \log \ex_i.
    \end{equation}
\end{thm}
\begin{proof}
    As $(\ea,\eb,\ec,\ed,\ee,\ef)$ are tetrahedral, the trace conditions (\cref{eq:tet_trace_conds}) necessarily imply that \cref{eq:balanced_sizes} holds.
    Therefore, by \cref{cor:harrow_ssa_lim} and the discussion surrounding the bound in \cref{eq:combined_bound} we conclude that along the convergent sequence of Young diagrams satisfies
    \begin{equation}
        I' \coloneqq \lim_{k\to\infty} \frac{1}{k} \log \left[\frac{\abs{\yb_k}!\abs{\ye_k}!}{\abs{\yc_k}!\abs{\yf_k}!}
        \frac{\dim(W_{\yc_k}) \dim(W_{\yf_k})}{\dim(W_{\yb_k}) \dim(W_{\ye_k})}\right] \geq 0
    \end{equation}
    provided the limit $I'$ exists.
    An application of Stirling's approximation \cite{robbins1955remark} to the dimension formula for $\dim(W_{\yx_k})$ together with the approximation $\yx_k \approx k \ex$ yields
    \begin{equation}
        \frac{1}{k}\log \left(\frac{\dim(W_{\yx_k})}{\abs{\yx}!}\right) = s(\ex) + \abs{\ex} (1-\log k) + O(k^{-1}\log k)
    \end{equation}
    where $s(\ex)$ is given in \cref{eq:little_s_defn}.
    Moreover by \cref{eq:balanced_sizes}, the $(1-\log k)$ terms cancel and therefore $I' = I(\ec, \ef; \eb, \ee) \geq 0$ as claimed above.
\end{proof}
